PREFACE QUIDED 

UIDED by the type of information sought daily from the judges and registers of probate, we have collected and arranged some of the procedural authorities bearing upon the administration of a decedent's estate in Michigan, and have included the petitions for the appointment of guardians. We have not attempted, however, to present a comprehensive survey of probate procedure. A large portion of the material concerns the proper preparation of various petitions; and, inasmuch as defective petitions have occasioned many collateral attacks in the past, it was deemed advisable to include a chapter upon the conclusiveness of probate decrees. The long quotations may be helpful in the preparation of memorandum briefs. The authors gratefully acknowledge their indebtedness to the Honorable Henry S. Hulbert for his many valuable suggestions and criticisms. 

Detroit, July, 1931. 

W. H. G. 

E. C. H. www.

PROCEDURAL PHASES 

1. 

OF 

2. 3. 

ESTATE ADMINISTRATION CHAPTER I The Administration of an Estate 

Many people are of the opinion that the estate of every decedent must be officially administered. In the absence of creditors the heirs or devisees and legatees, if of age and otherwise legally competent, may make an amicable division of the property. Such a course, however, is generally not advisable. ¡HEN a man dies the law not only makes available his real and personal property for the payment of his debts, but insures an orderly distribution through a proceeding in the probate court.¹

The administration of an estate consists of a series of separate proceedings which, with few exceptions, are initiated by petition, heard upon notice, and terminated by final order. A defect in any part of one proceeding, whether it occurs in the petition, notice, or order, may vitiate one or all succeeding adjudications. To a large extent the circumstances in the individual estate dictate the procedure, which is reasonably flexible and, if properly employed, will enable an efficient and early settlement of an estate.

How to Proceed With Administration 

The probate court is not authorized to commence the administration of an estate upon its own initiative. Generally the jurisdiction of the court is invoked by a written petition, which must be presented by a legally interested person, or by his agent, duly authorized.

The Judicature Act defines the jurisdiction of the probate court. 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15519; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13764.

Filing Petition Proof of Service 

After a petition is properly prepared, signed, and verified, it should be taken to the office of the probate court at which time a date of hearing may be obtained from the probate register or his deputy. During the same day, or soon thereafter, a clerk of the court delivers the petition to the judge in chambers who decides whether notice of the hearing shall be given to the interested parties personally or by publication in the newspaper. A notation of the petitioner's preference for a certain newspaper may be made upon the cover of the petition, though the judge is at liberty to disregard the suggestion. The probate office not only prepares the order of notice, but, upon its being signed by the judge, delivers a copy to the sheriff or newspaper, depending upon the type of service.

Upon the day assigned for a hearing, proof of service must be submitted to the court in the form of a certificate of service, signed by the sheriff or his deputy who made personal service, or, if service was made by publication, a printed copy of the notice taken from the paper in which it was published, together with an affidavit signed by the printer of the paper, his foreman, or principal clerk. Generally the newspaper voluntarily prepares an affidavit which may be obtained from one of its representatives prior to the hearing. In some counties space is provided for the newspapers in the corridor of the court, enabling petitioners to obtain affidavits upon paying the costs of publication; in others, the affidavits are left with the register or clerk of the court for delivery upon payment. If service was made by the sheriff, he will file his 4.

Sec. 12441, see Sec. 12425. The Records of the Probate Court 

After a paper is left at the probate office an endorsement of the date of filing is made upon the cover. If it is the first paper filed in the administration of an estate, it is given a calendar number. A record of this number and the name of the estate is entered in a minute book or short book. If the paper involves a hearing, a date is assigned and a record of the name and number of the case is made in another book called a docket. A separate page in the docket is set aside for each day assigned for hearing causes. Experience in each jurisdiction determines the number of the cases that may be heard conveniently during one day. All other cases above the allotted number are listed for the next succeeding day assigned for holding court. The paper is then placed in a file. 

The classification of all files is numerical, using the calendar number given the estate or proceeding. An index of the files is maintained according to the name, number and nature of the estate, i. e., minor, incompetent, deceased, et cetera. The short book contains a record, day by day, of all papers filed and orders entered. The entries in the short book are transferred to a book called the calendar in which they are recorded in the order of dates, under the file number in the estate of the deceased, or ward to which they pertain. The calendar is therefore the key to the court record and discloses under the title and number of each estate and in chronological order of dates every petition, appointment, or order. In examining a probate record it is the first book to be consulted after the file number is obtained from the index of estates. 6.

Wills are placed in a safe prior to their probate after which copies are recorded in a register of wills. The original wills are then placed in the estate file. Bonds are kept in a safe during the entire administration; copies are recorded in a register of bonds. Inventories, commissioners' reports, and accounts 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 13881; C. L. (1915) Sec. 12247.

Short Book and Calendar 

Where Papers are Filed

Record of

Orders are copied in separate registers, after which the originals are placed in the estate files. Original petitions, affidavits, and reports of sales and mortgages are recorded in registers of sales and mortgages. The orders or licenses to sell or mortgage and the orders of confirmation are recorded in the sale journal.'

In most jurisdictions a notation of the substance of the order granted is made upon the cover of the petition and signed by the judge. This notation identifies the petition upon which the order is based and furnishes the information for the preparation of a fully printed order which is signed by the judge and recorded in a book called the journal. The clerk of the court also makes a brief record of the order in the minute or short book, and from this information a record of the order is entered in the calendar. 

Special Administration 

The property of a decedent may need immediate protection and management. In urgent cases a petition requesting the appointment of a special administrator may be presented to the judge in chambers who may enter an order with or without notice. Sufficient testimony must be taken to establish the jurisdiction and satisfy the court as to the amount of the bond required for the protection of creditors and heirs. If the petitioner testifies erroneously upon any jurisdictional matter, the court may vacate the appointment within ninety days upon

An Allowance of Money for the Widow and Children

Following a man's death, his widow and children are generally in need of immediate financial assistance, which may be obtained upon the presentation of a petition, as soon as letters of administration have been issued to either a special or general administrative officer. Written notice of the time set for hearing the petition must be given to the administrative officer five days prior to the hearing in order that the estate may be represented." The widow's petition requesting an allowance may also request her articles of apparel, household furniture, and other personal property, which are not considered assets in the hands of the administrative officer. The statute provides: "The widow, if any, shall be allowed all her articles of apparel and ornaments, and all wearing apparel and ornaments of the deceased, and the household furniture of the deceased, and other personal property, to be selected by her, not exceeding in value $200.00, and the allowance shall be made as well when the widow waives the provision made for her in the will of her husband as when he dies intestate." Ordinary Procedure in Administration 

In the absence of an emergency, no call is made upon the probate court until it is determined whether or not the decedent left a will. If the person having custody of the will refuses to surrender it to the interested parties or to the probate court, official force may be employed."

Proceeding With Administration During an Appeal 

An appeal may be taken from the allowance or disallowance of a will or the appointment of an administrative officer. A will contest may be certified by the probate judge to the circuit court for trial. An appeal suspends all activity pursuant to the order appealed, and a certification has the same effect." Circumstances may demand that administration be proceeded with until the appeal is terminated. Statutory provision is therefore made for the appointment of a special administrator who may be given the powers of a general administrator.20

Additional Orders Generally Entered on the Appointment of an Administrative Officer

Inventory 

An inventory of the real estate and of all the goods, chattels, rights, and credits of the decedent should be filed within thirty days after the appointment of an executor or administrator." 

Liability accrues upon an administrative bond if the inventory is not filed within three months." The inventory should contain: The designation of the court; the name of the estate; the name and official title of the administrative officer; a recital that the listed assets constitute all of the property that has come to the possession or knowledge of the administrative officer; an itemized list of all the goods, chattels, rights and credits of the decedent-mortgages and notes as well as "vendor's interests" in land contracts should be described in detail; a full legal description of all real estate, including all land the decedent was purchasing upon contract, together with a detailed description of all outstanding mortgages and liens; and partnership interests." The inventory should be signed and verified by the administrative officer, and then turned over to the appraisers. 

Appraisal of Estate

The appraisers are required to set down opposite to each item in the inventory, distinctly in figures, the value thereof in money, and deliver the same, certified by them, together with letters of their appointment to the executor or administrator." Ordinarily a copy of the inventory is given to each appraiser to be used as a work sheet prior to filling out the original. 33. appointment of commissioners on claims when letters testamentary or of administration shall be granted. Duties of Administrative Officer 

It is the duty of the executor or administrator to collect all of the assets of the estate and, if necessary, he may sue at law or bring a bill in equity." The statute enables a discovery of the assets by a proceeding in the probate court." A contract to convey land made by a decedent may be specifically enforced in the probate court upon the presentation of a petition by an executor or administrator or by the person entitled to a conveyance by virtue of the contract." If full payment is received upon a land contract owned by the decedent, the executor or administrator may execute a deed or, if the purchaser is in default, forfeit the contract; action in either case may be undertaken without an express authorization from the court." All claims due the estate may be compromised upon obtaining the approval of the judge of probate,' while claims against the estate cannot be compromised by the administrative officer unless they have been previously ad- 35. 38

49. 

Elections by Widow

A widow may elect to take her dower and homestead rights or, as an alternative, her share of the real estate that she would have inherited if her husband had died intestate, in preference to the real estate devised in her husband's will;" or if her husband died intestate, she may petition for her dower and homestead rights rather than take her statutory share of the real estate as an heir of her husband.50 In testacy cases she may elect within a year of her husband's 50.

Under this provision the widow is entitled to her homestead as against collateral kindred whether the estate is solvent or insolvent and even though she elects to take under her husband's will, which orders the executor to sell all of the real estate. 

Koster v. Gellen (1900) 124 Mich. 149. 

In Dei v. Habel (1879) 41 Mich. 88 the decedent left a young

Ichild and widow. The child died within a few months after the father. The re-marriage of the widow terminated her homestead right.

Upon the termination of a homestead, the creditors may delay too long to request its sale. In re Emmon's Estate (1905) 142 

Mich. 299. "The homestead of the family, after the death of the owner thereof, shall be exempt from the payment of his debts in all cases during the minority of his children." Mich. Const. Art.

XIV. Sec. 3. 

"The homestead right does not attach in favor of a widow and children unless the estate is insolvent and in debt; and if it is not, it goes to the heirs at once, subject to the widow's right of dower." Syllabus in Zoellner v. Zoellner (1884) 53 Mich.

Accounts "It is the design of our laws that our probate records shall furnish all requisite information relating to the estates of deceased persons, in order that heirs, creditors, and all others interested may resort to them with confidence, for the purpose of ascertaining their respective rights, and the course of administration."""1 In keeping with this design the statute provides that an administrative officer shall annually file an account and at such other times as may be directed by the probate judge."

The words "assign" and "distribute" are generally used indiscriminately. Money is "distributed." An automobile is "assigned." The use of the word "assign" in connection with real estate is apt to be misleading. In case of intestacy the heirs become vested of the title of real estate at the date of the decedent's death by virtue of the statute of descents. 

The order of "assignment" determines the names of the heirs who inherit and the proportion or part that each takes, and further, that all debts and obligations of the deceased have been paid or satisfied." Title acquired by will is validated by an admission of the will to probate plus an order of "assignment" which, if properly drawn, fixes as a finality the status of the title devised, or in other words, construes the 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15728; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13915. 

In Calhoun v. Cracknell (1918) 202 Mich. 430 the court said (p. 435) "The distribution (assignment) does not transfer property, but is merely declaratory. In partition, one having an undivided interest in the whole of the real estate is invested by the partition proceedings with the whole title of part thereof. 

Distribution differs from partition in that the former transfers no title, but merely declares the persons upon whom the law casts the succession, and the extent of their respective interests." See, however, the language in Mote v. Lippert (1930) 251 Mich. 49, found on pages 52, 53: "The final order (of assignment) in regard to real estate has the force and effect of a decree of partition, and ex proprie vigore, accomplishes a transfer of the real estate. 

However, the order is merely declaratory as to the persons who are entitled to personal property, and it requires an additional act by the executor or administrator in order to carry out this order." While the case was properly decided, it is submitted that this language does not accurately state the law concerning the assignment of real estate. "Assign" and "Distribute"

While an administrative officer may distribute and assign all of the personal property after the payment of the debts without an order of the court," it is customary to present a final account together with a petition requesting the distribution and assignment of the estate in order to gain the protection afforded by a court order. "A decree of (assignment or) distribution, if properly made after due notice, is in its nature final, and unless set aside for fraud . . . or appealed from within the time limited by law, it concludes the rights of all parties interested in the estate, and generally can not be impeached in a collateral proceeding." 80 A petition for the assignment and distribution of the estate should contain a brief recital of all administration steps in order to assist the court at the hearing. A prayer that the property be distributed and assigned according to the terms of a will or statute should never be employed. The statute requires that the decree specifically name the parties and the interests to which they are entitled." If any of the heirs or

Ordinarily the assets are remitted to the jurisdiction of the domicile if the time there allowed for the presentation of claims has not expired, and this is always done upon a showing that there are not sufficient assets in the domicile to satisfy creditors. CHAPTER II The Necessity of Administration The following dictum by Justice Cooley in Brown v. 

Forsche' is often cited: (p. 500) "Formal proceedings for the settlement of an estate are never necessary if all parties concerned can agree to dispense with them. This is often done, and the expense of administration thereby avoided. . . . Family arrangements for this purpose, it is said are favorites of the law, and when fairly made are never allowed to be disturbed by the parties, or by any others for them."

In Needham v. Gillett' the heirs were held to their agreement. The entire estate consisted of a mortgage and note made by the decedent's brother. By agreement, he paid the funeral expenses and gave the heirs their share of the estate. 

Later the probate court appointed an administrator, which appointment was set aside. The court said: (pp. 575, 576) "Whether, under the agreed facts, the settlement made between the heirs-at-law. . . constitutes no legal bar to the granting of administration upon (the decedent's) estate, as claimed, or not, yet such settlement ought to and would equitably estop them from afterwards attempting to open or disturb it. It is agreed that there are no creditors or others interested in the matter, and that the mortgage and note. . . represented all the property, of which (the decedent) was possessed . . 

Under such circumstances, where all the heirs-at-law, being of full age, meet and agree upon a settlement and division of the property, and the same is in good faith carried out, it would be strange indeed if they could retain what they had received, being all the law would have given them under any course 

1. 43 Mich. 492 (1880). 

2. 39 Mich. 574 (1878). 

In Shurte v. Fletcher (1896) 111 Mich. 84, 98, 99 the court held as stated in the syllabus: "Refusal of the probate court to appoint an administrator on the ground that the estate has been settled by an agreement of the heirs is not res judicata as to the validity of such agreement, as the court could not decree specific performance of the agreement if valid, or cancel it if invalid." 27

Objection to

Family Settlements of settlement by an administrator, and still commence proceedings to recover the same again." 993 The main practical objection to such family agreements was mentioned by Justice Cooley, as he continued his opinion in Brown v. Forsche: (p. 501) "No doubt such a settlement will be subject to the rights of creditors in any case where the statutory proceedings have not been taken to bar their claim; but when these have been had, the right to take the property under voluntary arrangement must be commensurate with the right to compel the administrator to distribute it." 

Woerner states: "The rights of creditors to the assets of a deceased person is usually the principal reason for requiring official administration, and courts, therefore, sanction the disposition of the property of a decedent without the appointment of an administrator where it is certain that no debts are owing.' 99 4 In the absence of an official determination, the heirs can never be certain, within a period of ten years, that their property is freed of creditors' liens. As stated by the court in 

Powell v. Palmer (p. 243) "From the nature of the case, the proposition, that there are no debts provable against the estate of a deceased person, is, therefore, a negative proposition, which is not susceptible of absolute proof. No evidence which could be offered in support of such a proposition could go further than to reach a strong degree of probability."" 

3. See Foote v. Foote (1886) 61 Mich. 181, 190; Letts v. Letts (1888) 73 Mich. 138, 143. 4. 

Woerner, American Law of Administration, 3d ed. Sec. 201. 

5. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15724, P. A. (1923) No. 5. 6.

7. In the case of In re Sprague's Estate (1900) 125 Mich. 357 the court said as dictum: (p. 364) "No one for eight years asked for administration upon (the decedent's) estate. Under the circumstances, it would be well-nigh absurd to hold that administration was necessary for the benefit of creditors. Creditors do not wait eight years without taking steps to enforce their claims against the estate of their deceased debtor." Two years later the court held that the same administration was unnecessary in 

Sprague v. Moore (1902) 130 Mich. 92, 105, 106.

In Ormsbee v. Piper" the heirs made a division agreement covering the decedent's estate in which was included a mortgage. The widow died in custody of the mortgage and it passed to her executor. A son had himself appointed administrator of his father's estate and successfully replevined the mortgage. It was objected in the suit that by virtue of Needham v. Gillett, administration was unnecessary. The court held (p. 268): "We cannot, in this collateral action, say that there was no necessity for the appointment of an administrator. That was a question for the probate court, and no appeal was taken. (and further on) In the case of Needham v. Gillett, . . . the question arose on appeal from the order appointing the administrator. (and on pp. 270, 271) Nowhere do we find that an administrator duly appointed without appeal can be denied the uncollected choses-in-action yet belonging to the estate, upon the ground that the heirs have agreed that there should be no administration of the estate." 

The agree ment did not transfer the custody or legal title of the mortgage to any of the parties." 8. 9.

Actions for the collection of debts must be brought by an administrator, or some one holding the legal title. The heirs can not enforce the payment of claims due the estate." 10. 

However, in Ogooshevitz v. Arnold (1917) 197 Mich. 203 the court said that there were but three methods of barring claims against an estate, namely the ten year statute of limitations, a presentation of claims before the probate court or commissioners on claims, and the closing of the estate. See Brobst v. Brobst (1916) 190 Mich. 63, 67. 

123 Mich. 265 (1900). 

In Diem v. Drogmiller (1909) 158 Mich. 380 the administrator, thirteen years after the decedent's death, was unsuccessful in recovering personal property from one of the heirs. It appeared that there were no debts and that the administration was started by a daughter who had no right to any share of the property to be recovered. For years all of the heirs had acquiesced in a family settlement. 

Abbott v. Godfroy's Heirs (1849) 1 Mich. 178, 185; Hollowell v.

71, 77; Morris v. Vyse (1908) 154 Mich. 253, 259. 

Heirs Cannot

Enforce Payment

In Brobst v. Brobst" the daughter of the deceased took an assignment of a note from other heirs and brought suit. The court said: (p. 67) “If the heirs and distributees of an estate were entitled to receive and enforce payment of debts due the estate whenever it was believed that there were no creditors, then every debtor would be under the necessity of determining at his peril, whether there were such creditors or not. There might be debts depending upon a contingency that had not happened, or incurred in a fiduciary relation not falling within the bar of the statute of limitations. . . Without attempting to name them all, there are certain exceptions to the rule that actions for the collection of debts due an estate must be brought by an administrator, or some one holding the legal title. The case of Letts v. Letts" illustrates such an exception. 

There the debtor had acknowledged the plaintiff's right to a note received by her from her father's estate, and had agreed to treat the moneys called for by the note as a loan from the plaintiff. But the bare fact that there are probably no creditors of the estate to which the note belongs, and that is the most that can be said in any case until some conclusive bar has intervened, does not create an exception in this state to the rule." 99 18 And for other reasons it is unwise to deal with the heirs 

11. 190 Mich. 63 (1916). 12. 

73 Mich. 138 (1888). 

13. See dictum in Ormsbee v. Piper (1900) 123 Mich. 265, 269, citing Woerner. (See American Law of Administration, 3d ed. Sec. 200 for authorities from many jurisdictions.)

People generally-upon the advice of counsel-refuse to deal with any one save a legally appointed administrator or executor, unless all possible liabilities are clearly defined in the law of the jurisdiction. Caution is justifiable upon the part of custodians of safe deposit vaults, banks, stock transfer agents, vendors, in title retaining contracts, and others in their refusal to deal with the heirs in the absence of administration. As a practical matter, therefore, administration proceedings will greatly convenience the heirs even though they may not be absolutely essential as a matter of legal theory under certain facts. Through administration the adjustment and satisfaction of estate taxes is greatly facilitated.

CHAPTER III Special Administration 

Frequently when a person dies his property is in immediate need of protection and management. A carload of tomatoes may have arrived from Texas, and unless someone is authorized to dispose of them quickly all will be lost. The same emergency may arise during an appeal from the allowance of a will, or the appointment of a general administrator. 

Under such circumstances the judge of probate is authorized to appoint a special administrator upon such notice as he may direct or without notice in his discretion. The form of petition for the appointment of a general administrator may be used for the appointment of a special administrator with two modifications. It should be alleged whether or not the deceased left a will. An additional provision reciting the necessity for the appointment is required.

The statute' provides "When by reason of delay in granting letters testamentary or of administration, or when from any other cause, the judge of probate deems it expedient so to do, he may after such notice as he may direct, or without notice in his discretion, appoint an administrator to act in collecting and taking charge of the estate of the deceased, until the executor or administrator shall be appointed; and no appeal shall be allowed from the appointment of such special administrator."

The statute is very broad and "indicates the intention of the legislature to give the probate judge almost absolute discretion as to whether a special administrator shall be appointed in any case.' 29 2 A special administrator "shall collect all the goods, chattels and debts of the deceased, and preserve the same for the executor or administrator who may afterwards be appointed, and for that purpose may commence and maintain actions as 1. 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15588; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13822. 

2. Grece v. Helm (1892) 91 Mich. 450, 455, 456. 

Discretion of Judge

Duties 33

All personal actions, the cause of which does by law survive and which may be pending either for or against the intestate of such special administrator, may be proceeded with and be prosecuted by or against such special administrator, and the same proceedings taken as are or may hereafter be provided by law relating to such actions in cases where an executor or general administrator has been appointed.' "3 In Union Trust Company v. In Zimmer v. Saier it was held that a special administrator had no authority to employ and pay an attorney out of the funds of the estate in a contest over the probate of a will by the heirs."

CHAPTER IV The Petition for Administration (Intestate) 

The petition for administration should be fairly and legibly written in ink or printed and should recite: 

1. The designation of the court; 

2. The character of the paper; 3.

The title of the estate; 4.

The name and address of the petitioner; 

5. The interest of the petitioner and his right to apply to the court; 

6. The death of the intestate and the date of his decease; 

7. That no will was left by the decedent; 

8. The domicile of the decedent at the time of his death; 

9. Whether he left property within the state of Michigan; and county or counties where located; 

10. The estimated value of the real estate; 

11. The estimated value of the personal property; 

12. The names, relationships, ages, and residences of the widow or widower, and the heirs-at-law; and whether any of the heirs are under a legal disability, and if so, the names and residences of their guardians, so far as known to the petitioner, or can with reasonable diligence be ascertained; 13.

A prayer that the administration of the estate be granted to the nominee of the petitioner or some other suitable person; 

14. The address of the nominated administrator.

The petition should be signed and verified by the petitioner.' 

1. Probate Court Rule III. 

36

Upon the cover of the petition there should be endorsed "State of Michigan, Probate Court for the County of blank," the name of the estate, the character of the petition, and the name of the attorney, if any, appearing for the petitioner.' 

The Explanation of the Recitals in the Petition The explanation of the recitals: 

1. The Designation of the Court; 

2. The Character of the Paper 3. The Title of the Estate. 

4. The Name and Address of the Petitioner. 

Rule VII of the Michigan Court Rules provides that all papers shall be entitled in the name of the court. A similar provision is lacking in the Probate Court Rules. The form petitions prepared by a committee of the Association of Michigan Judges of Probate in 1902 contain the usual venue form- State of Michigan, County of blank-employed in an affidavit, and the recital "Probate Court for said County." 2. 3.

The character of the paper and the title of the estate, or proceeding, must be alleged as provided in Rule I of the Probate Court Rules. The approved probate form contains a space for the address of the petitioner.

His Right to Apply to the Court.

Not only does the statute specify the persons entitled to administer an estate, but as construed by the court, it defines the persons entitled to present a petition for administration.®

7.

In construing the statute in the case of Shipman v. Butterfield,' the court said: (p. 488) "The petition in this case does not show that it is made by any person interested in the settlement of the estate as the statute requires. (And further on in the opinion.) It is true that the statute does not in express terms require that these facts (petitioner's interest) shall be set out in the petition, but the rule is universal that jurisdiction must affirmatively appear..."

Rule III of the Probate Court Rules provides that a person making a petition "shall state the facts with common certainty, which show the jurisdiction of the court and the interest of the petitioner and his right to apply to the court." 

Therefore, since the interest of the petitioner is a jurisdictional fact, as held in Shipman v. Butterfield, it must be alleged in the petition. 

47 Mich. 487, 488 (1882). 

The holding in Shipman v. Butterfield is criticized in the chapter on Collateral Attack (see p. 149). It did not appear upon the face of the petition whether the petitioner was properly interested or not as happened in the cases of Haug v. Primeau (1893) 98 Mich. 91, 95, and Breen v. Pangborn (1883) 51 Mich. 

29. Upon collateral attack, it should have been presumed that the probate court found, as a fact, that the petitioner was a properly interested party under the statute. The orders of the probate court are entitled to presumptions of regularity, if the record is silent as to jurisdictional matters. 8. 

In Wilkinson v. Conaty (1887) 65 Mich. 614 the court said as dicta: (p. 621) "But what are jurisdictional facts? They are that the person whose estate is to be administered died intestate, and was, at the time of his death, either an inhabitant or resident of the same county in which application is made, or, if he died without the state, that he left an estate in the county to be administered. If these facts appear, the court has jurisdiction to appoint an administrator upon the petition of a party interested. 

The other matters usually contained in a petition are matters of practice designed to inform the court, and enable it to fix a proper time for hearing, and determine what notice is proper to be given."

The statute provides that in case any one of the preferred class fails to petition for administration within thirty days after the death of the intestate, administration may be granted to one or more of the principal creditors. In Wilkinson v. Conaty, it was objected that the petitioner did not allege the fact of his being a principal creditor, or that the widow or next of kin had failed to file a petition for administration. The court said: (p. 620) "The statute does not require the petition for administration to be made by the principal creditors of the deceased. . ., any creditor may apply to . . . have the estate administered. The court, acting upon such application, may grant administration to one or more of the principal creditors. If the petition is made by a creditor it need not state that no application has been made by the widow or next of kin, for the court will take judicial notice of its own files and records . . .” 

The statute provides that administration may be granted to the widow, husband, (widower) or next of kin, or a grantee of the interest of one or more of them. 

In Osmun v. Galbraith," a daughter's trustee in bankruptcy was considered a grantee of the decedent's next of kin. 

In the case of In re Corby's Estate," the court held that an incompetent, or infant next of kin, did not lose her right to petition for the nomination of an administrator for her father's estate, since the right of the infant or incompetent could be invoked by the general guardian." 

Under certain circumstances a person entitled to administration may not only lose his right to those of lesser preference, but even to persons not mentioned in the statute. 6. The Death of the Intestate and The Date of His Decease. 

By virtue of Rule III of the Probate Court Rules, the court's jurisdiction must be shown in the petition. The death of the intestate is of prime importance jurisdictionally. Administration of an estate of a living person is void." 

125 Mich. 357 (1900). 

In Beckwith v. Bates (1924) 228 Mich. 400, the court stated the question involved: (p. 401) "Has the probate court jurisdiction to administer the estate of one presumed by long time absence to be dead, but in fact alive?" It was held that the probate court, for want of subject matter, had no jurisdiction to administer and distribute the property.

The court cited with approval the case of Scott v. McNeal (1893) 154 U. S. 34, which held that (quoting from the syllabus) "A court of probate, in the exercise of its jurisdiction over the probate of wills and the administration of estates of deceased persons, has no jurisdiction to appoint an administrator of the estate of a living person; and its orders, made after public notice, appointing an administrator of the estate of a person, who is in fact alive, although he has been absent and not heard from for seven years, and licensing the administrator to sell his land for payment of his debts, are void, and the purchaser at the sale takes no title as against him."

If reasonable and ample provision is made for the protection of the absentee, absentee administration is constitutional. Walz v.

1 25. 

The date of the decedent's death is not jurisdictional,' though the court has said that administration is entirely unnecessary, if there are no creditors." It nevertheless has an important bearing upon other jurisdictional matters. Creditors may petition only after a period of thirty days. If some of the next of kin have recently died, comparisons must be made with the date of their death in order to determine who are the next of kin entitled to petition. 26. 

7. That No Will was Left by the Decedent.

Of course, if there be a will, administration would be granted to no one-save a special administrator-until the will was admitted to probate; and then administration would be granted to the party nominated by the testator, if the nominee was qualified. If not qualified, or no one was named, the appointment would probably fall to one of the most interested beneficiaries under the will." By statute it is provided that "If, after the granting of letters of administration by any probate court, on the estate of any deceased person, as if he had died intestate, a will of such deceased person shall be duly proved and allowed by such court, the first administration shall, by decree of said court, be revoked, and the powers of the administrator shall cease, and he shall thereupon surrender 27. 25

By statute, each judge of probate is given jurisdiction "Of all matters relating to the settlement of the estates of all deceased persons, whether testate or intestate, who were at the time of their decease inhabitants of, or residents in, his county, and of all who shall die without the state leaving any estate within such county to be administered." It is further provided by statute that "When any person shall die intestate, being an inhabitant of this state, letters of administration of his estate shall be granted by the probate court of the county of which he was an inhabitant, or resident, at the time of his death; if such deceased person, at the time of his death, resided in any other state or country, leaving estate to be administered in the state, administration thereof shall be granted by the probate court of any county in which there shall be estate to be administered; and the administration first legally granted shall extend to all the estate of the deceased in this state, and shall exclude the jurisdiction of the probate court of every other county." 9 30 

In the statutes the words "inhabitant" or "resident," are used; while in case law the term "domicile" is employed. 10 and 11. The Estimated Value of the Real 

Estate; The Estimated Value of the Personal

Property. 

The form of petition adopted by the probate judges requires an estimate of the value of both real and personal property. 

If an administrator is appointed the court will fix the amount of the required bond. A knowledge of the amount of personal property that will be administered is therefore essential. 

A knowledge of the approximate amount of the real estate and whether or not it is income producing property, may have a bearing upon the discretion used in appointing an administrator, especially if there is very little personal property. 

12. The Names, Relationships, Ages, and 

Residences of the Widow, Widower and the

Heirs-at-Law; Whether any of the Heirs are 

Under a Legal Disability, and, if so, the 

Names and Residences of their Guardians so far as Known to the Petitioner, or can with Reasonable Diligence be Ascertained. 

Probate Court Rule IV provides that a petition for administration shall state the names and residences of the widow and heirs-at-law of the deceased, who, if any, are minors, or otherwise under disability, and the names and residences of their guardians, if any, in this state, so far as known to the petitioner, or can with reasonable diligence be ascertained. 

Probate Court Rule III provides that all petitions to institute any proceedings in the court, when such facts do not already appear of record in the case, shall state whether any persons under the age of twenty-one years, or otherwise incapable of prosecuting or defending themselves, have any interest in the subject matter thereof, which may be affected by the order thereon, and the name, age, and residence of in the nature of a bill of peace to have the title adjudicated of either real or personal property if worth more than one hundred dollars. 13. A Prayer that the Administration of the 

Estate may be Granted to the Nominee of the 

Petitioner, or Some Other Suitable Person. 

It has been said that a prayer indicates "the desire of the petitioner as to some particular adjudication which he wishes the court to make," but fixes "a limit beyond which the court is not authorized to go in making its judicial award.' 99 39 The court may, in its discretion or as required by law, appoint some other party than the nominee of the petitioner as administrator. Therefore, the recital in the petition of "or some other suitable person" is appropriate. 

It is provided by statute that "Administration of the estate of a person dying intestate shall be granted to some one or more of the persons hereinafter mentioned, and they shall be respectively entitled to the same in the following order: "1. The widow, husband, or next of kin, or a grantee of the interest of one or more of them, or such of them as the judge of probate may think proper, or such person or persons as the widow, husband, next of kin, or grantee may request to have appointed, if suitable and competent to discharge the trust; "2. If the widow, husband, next of kin, or grantee, or the person selected by them shall be unsuitable or incompetent, or if the widow, husband, next of kin or grantee shall neglect for thirty days after the death of the intestate to apply for administration, or to request that administration be granted to some other person, the same may be granted to one or more of the

A husband or wife loses all rights in each other's estate, if at the time of death the survivor is guilty of bigamy." It was argued in the case of In re Dettman's Estate that a widow was not entitled to administration because she had lived with a man in Toledo some fourteen years before her husband's death, though at the time she was the wife of the decedent. In advising the probate court to appoint the widow as administratrix, the supreme court said: (p. 234) "Bigamy means more than the meretricious relation of one who has a husband or wife living. It imports a form or ceremony of marriage by a spouse who has a lawful wife or husband. In McMeekin v. Saginaw Probate Judge," after the thirty day period had elapsed, two creditors petitioned for the appointment of X as administrator, claiming a right to dictate the appointment, over the objection of the next of kin and other creditors. The petitioning creditors did not claim that their nominee was a principal creditor. The court held that even though, by way of assumption, the next of kin could not be heard, both sets of creditors were on the same footing and therefore the appointment of Y, as a co-administrator with X, was proper. 59.

In the case of In re Morgan's Estate," a son petitioned for the appointment of his brother F, five days after his father's death. He alleged that his step-mother was not a competent person to administer. Two months later F petitioned for his own appointment, also alleging the incompetency of his stepmother. Four months after the first petition was filed, the widow prayed in a cross-petition for her own appointment, alleging that F could not administer disinterestedly because of financial reasons. The probate court appointed F. The supreme court held this improper. The widow had the right to administration if she was mentally competent, even though not as experienced as others in business. It was argued that the widow lost her right by failing to make timely application. 

The court said: (pp. 73, 74) "Whether the widow would have a right to insist on her appointment after the creditors had properly instituted probate proceedings in compliance with (the statute), is not involved here. The contest is between the widow and the son, following a petition filed by an heir within five days after the death of the intestate, 60. 61. 

In Smith v. Lurty (1907) 107 Va. 548, 59 S. E. 403, the widow, who had the first right to administration, was not appointed because "she was very hostile to, and not upon speaking terms with the principal devisee and legatee." Of course, there was no question of delay in the case.

The statute applies to the situation at the time letters of administration are granted, and not to the situation at the time of the death of the deceased.

CHAPTER V The Petition for Administration (Testate) 

The petition shall be fairly and legibly written in ink or printed and should recite: 

1. The designation of the court; 

2. The character of the paper; 

3. The title of the estate; 

4. The name and address of the petitioner; 

5. The interest of the petitioner and his right to apply to the court; 

6. The death of the testator and the date of his decease; 

7. That the paper filed with the petition constitutes the last will of the deceased; 

8. The domicile of the deceased at the time of his death; 

9. Whether he left property within the state of Michigan; and the county or counties where located; 

10. The estimated value of the real estate; 

11. The estimated value of the personal property; 

12. The names, relationships, ages, and residences of the widow or widower, and the heirs-at-law and whether any of the heirs are under a legal disability, and if so, the names and residences of their guardians, so far as known to the petitioner, or can with reasonable diligence be ascertained; 

13. A prayer that the will may be admitted to probate; 

14. A prayer that the administration of the estate be granted to the party named by the petitioner, or some other suitable person; 

15. The address of the executor or nominated administrator with will annexed.

The petition should be signed and verified by the petitioner.'

Explanation of the Recitals in the Petition The explanation of the recitals: 

1. The Designation of the Court; 

2. The Character of the Paper; 

3. The Title of the Estate;

Rule VII of the Michigan Court Rules provides that all papers shall be entitled in the name of the court. A similar provision is lacking in the Probate Court Rules. The form petitions, prepared by a committee of the association of the Michigan Judges of Probate in 1902, contain the usual venue form-State of Michigan, County of blank-employed in the affidavit, and the recital "Probate Court for said County." 

The character of the paper and the title of the estate, or proceeding, must be alleged as provided in Rule I of the Probate Court Rules. The approved probate form contains a space for the address of the petitioner. 2. 3.

Heir of 

Devisee or Legatee Petition by Agent should be granted to those beneficially entitled-though not to the exclusion of the next of kin, or creditors, as an alternative. 

Therefore, since the next of kin or creditors may be entitled to administration, it follows that they should be entitled to petition for the probate of a will. There is no statute providing that either the beneficiaries or next of kin shall be entitled to administration in case no executor is named in the will, however, its absence is undoubtedly immaterial, as the right has always been recognized. If such a statute were enacted it would probably, in this regard, follow the above statute in case the executor refuses his trust. 

If the will is denied probate, the next of kin will be interested by virtue of the intestacy statute. However, it seems awkward to justify one's interest in petitioning for probate on the ground that he will beneficially take if the will is denied. 

As long as the next of kin or creditors are entitled to petition for administration, no other reason need be assigned in justification. 

Apparently under the rule of Scripps v. Wayne Probate

Judge' an heir may force the issue of will or no will by petitioning for intestate administration. 

An heir of a devisee or legatee, dying after the testator, should be an interested petitioner. Likewise a legatee or devisee of such party, even though the second will has not been probated. Certainly the issue of a legatee, predeceasing the testator, would constitute a properly interested class under section 15552, unless it is clear that they are barred in the will or are not of the blood of the testator. If the will needs an interpretation to decide whether they take, it would seem fair to regard them as properly interested for the purpose of filing a petition.

Study and compare. In Estate of Edwards (1908) 154 Cal. 91, the question presented and its solution was stated thus by the court: (p. 92) "Where it is made to appear that an instrument testamentary in character and executed with the formalities required by law has not been offered for probate, may a court, upon application for general letters of administration, hear and determine the question of the validity or invalidity of this instrument, and, as it shall determine, grant, or refuse to grant general letters of administration; or does it become the duty of the court, upon such a showing, to postpone the consideration of the application for letters of administration until, in the appropriate proceeding provided by law for the proving of a will, the question of the validity or invalidity of the instrument shall have been determined? In fact, the latter was the course which the (lower) court adopted, and its determination we think was sound."

Concerning the effect of the California Code provisions upon the question, the court said: (p. 93) "Where an instrument is in form a will, there will usually be somebody interested in maintaining its validity who will therefore petition the court to have it proved. Under our procedure such a petition seems necessary to confer upon the court jurisdiction to act (Code Civ. Proc., Secs. 1299 et seq.) though under our earlier probate procedure no petition was required. (In re Howard's Estate, 22 Cal. 395)

Manifestly the petition for letters of administration in such a case as this, is presented by one in hostility to the asserted will. 

The notice required to be given (Code Civ. Proc., Sec. 1373) is an entirely different notice from that required to be given upon petition for the proving of a will (Code Civ. Proc., Secs. 1303, 1304). No notice is given to the executors, devisees, or legatees, or to any person who may be interested in supporting the alleged will. Upon the hearing therefore it may be assumed that no evidence will be forthcoming to substantiate the validity of the will,

It is provided by statute that, "When any will shall have been delivered into or deposited in any probate court having jurisdiction of the same, such court shall appoint a time and place for proving it, when all concerned may appear and contest the probate of the will, and shall cause public notice thereof to be given by service as provided in section nine of this chapter; and no will shall be proved until notice shall be given as herein provided." 99 17 It would not be doing violence to the language of the above section to conclude that the judge of probate is empowered to commence the probate proceeding upon his own initiative, even though entirely contrary to practice. It has been stated (without citation of authorities) that: "In many states the judge of probate or register of wills is, by statute, made the custodian of wills deposited with him. . . In such states, it is his duty, as soon as he receives information of the death of any testator whose will he has in custody, to institute proceedings for the probate thereof, and to that end compel the attendance of the necessary witnesses to prove its execution and the death of the testator." 99 18 19 A slight reference to the practice is disapproved in the dicta of a Maine decision in which the court, after a preliminary discussion of the importance of the statutes enabling a public inspection of a will, said: (pp. 292, 293) “The will, having been presented, may or may not be probated. There has been some discussion, in the cases, (referring probably, among other cases, to Stebbins v. Lathrop which was cited in the brief) as to the extent of the discretionary powers of the 20 while evidence against that validity will be offered. The result may be foreseen."

6. The Death of the Testator and The Date of His Decease. 

By virtue of Rule III of the Probate Court Rules, the court's jurisdiction must be shown in the petition. A petition to probate a will is lifeless without an allegation of death.

In Lloyd v. Wayne Circuit Judge," the Michigan court held inoperative a statute" providing for the ante mortem probate of a will.

The date of death may be jurisdictional." The fact that it aids in the determination of jurisdictional matters is a sufficient warrant for its presence in the petition. For instance, the propriety of the petitioner's interest when claiming under terested in a will, if the executor refuses to exhibit it, is the statute penalty. But this position cannot be maintained. The statute remedy is cumulative and does not take away the rights of any party claiming under the will, nor the jurisdiction of the judge of probate." (and further on) "It is said, the judge may ex officio, or at the instance of any one, cite the executor to prove the will, because the applicant may be ignorant of the contents of the will, and may expect a legacy, and has a right to be informed. 3 Bac. Abr. 40, Executors & c. E, 8; Godolph, 60. Besides, the legatees or devisees may be absent or unknown; in which cases it is proper for the judge of probate to proceed ex officio, and to prevent the concealment, suppression, or loss of the will. And who can be injured by such an exercise of his authority? Certainly no one; for still the legatee, if he sees fit, may renounce his legacy." The above statements were dicta. A creditor of the testator, executor, and principal devisee was held to be a proper party to have the will exhibited for probate. 4 

Pick (Mass.) 33, 42 (1826). 

56 Mich. 236, 239, 240 (1885). 21. 

22. P. A. (1883) No. 25. 23.

If the legatee predeceased the testator, a limited class -possibly his issue" would become properly interested petitioners; while death shortly after the testator might involve a much larger class-his heirs. Obviously the judge of probate must know the date of death in order to determine whether thirty days have elapsed, if a creditor petitions.

Anyone having custody of a will should deliver it either to the probate judge or the executor therein named within thirty days after learning of the testator's death." If the will is in the custody of the judge, obviously a recital to that effect would be substituted for the printed formula that the will is filed with the petition. 

An allegation, that the paper for which probate is prayed constitutes the last will of the testator, rather than any other will, is obviously essential. It is not necessary to allege testamentary capacity. In the case of In re Hathaway's Appeal,” on an appeal to the circuit court on the allowance of a will to probate, the contestant's counsel asked leave to demur to the petition presented for probate for the reason that it did not aver the testamentary capacity of the testatrix, nor that she was of sound mind and memory. The court held that "the issues were inherent (will or no will) and it was not a necessary expedient to attempt to reduce them into written forms. The inquiry can neither be enlarged nor contracted by pleadings." 31. 32. 

8 and 9. The Domicile of the Deceased at the 

Time of His Death; Whether He Left Property Within the State of Michigan, and the 

County or Counties Where Located. 

By statute each judge of probate is given jurisdiction "of all matters relating to the settlement of the estates of all deceased persons, whether testate or intestate, who were at the time of their decease a grandson, who casually came into possession of a will, was not liable to a legatee for failure to offer it for probate. The grandson presented it for probate within a period of seven to ten years after he came upon the will. After his grandfather's death, he found it upon his bed in his father's home and, upon reading it, filed it away. 

The court said: (p. 48) "Frequently men accept the custody of wills-lawyers, bankers and others who act in those capacities are frequently called upon to receive and accept wills and keep them in their safes or strong boxes or safe-deposit vaults for safe-keeping for the men who made them, and it is those persons that this statute was aimed at, and not a person who might casually come into possession of a will some time afterward, as occurred in this case.' See In re Keene's Estate (1918) 202 Mich. 646, 656.

67 (1.) inhabitants of, or residents in his county, and (2.) of all who shall die without the state leaving any estate within such county to be administered." " 

In the statute the words "inhabitant" or "resident" are used while in case law, the term "domicile" is employed. The word residence is "used with many shades of meaning, from the merest temporary presence to the most permanent abode;" " however, as concerns probate jurisdiction for the settlement of estates, the statutory words are synonymous with domicile in denoting a permanent residence."

Apparently under this statute" the probate court is given jurisdiction to probate the will of any one leaving property within the state, though domiciled elsewhere, and such is the rule in most states. The Supreme Court has held otherwise, in the case of In re Corning's Estate." In that case A died testate, having lived in Saginaw, Michigan, for about a year previous to his death, and left there a mortgage upon land in Saginaw County. His domicile was in New York City. 33. 34. 35. 36.

The court construed the statutes and found that the “legislature has recognized the right of the courts of the domicile ... to conclusively determine the validity of a will" (originally, as distinguished from an ancillary proceeding) and that accordingly the probate court of Saginaw had no jurisdiction to probate the will of A. Ostrander, J., said (p. 477): Assuming the right of each state to assert complete jurisdiction in rem over all property of decedent's found within the state, including the right to determine, through its tribunals, the validity or non-validity of a foreign will, it is equally the right 37. 38. 88 66

In 1921 the legislature enacted the following: "The will of any deceased person domiciled without the state of Michigan leaving any estate within the state of Michigan, which shall not have been filed or presented for probate and allowance in the jurisdiction of the domicile of such deceased person, may be delivered into the probate court any county in this state in which there may be an estate of such deceased person to be administered and thereupon the same proceedings may be had in relation to the probate of such will and the administration of such estate as though such deceased person had been an inhabitant of or resident in such county, and with like force and effect as to all estate of such deceased person within the state of Michigan. "The will of any deceased person domiciled within the state of Michigan which shall have been filed for or presented for probate and allowance in any jurisdiction other than the domicile of such deceased person, may be established and probated in the probate court of the county in this state in which the testator was domiciled, by duly filing an exemplified copy of said will and a petition praying for the probate of such will, which petition shall contain the names of the subscribing witnesses and if living, their places of residence, together with the names and residences of all known persons who have personal knowledge of the execution of said will.

When this statute was first enacted, no presumptions of regularity would be indulged, if the record of a probate court was silent as to jurisdictional matters. 10 and 11. The Estimated Value of the Real Estate.

And The Estimated Value of the Personal Estate. 

The form of petition adopted by the probate judges requires an estimate of the value of both real and personal property. 

If the prayer of the petitioner is granted-the will admitted to probate and the executor or administrator with the will annexed appointed-it is customary to fix the amount of the required bond in the order of appointment. A knowledge of the amount of personal property that will be administered is therefore essential.

A knowledge of the approximate amount of the real estate and whether or not it is income producing property may have a bearing upon the discretion used in appointing an administrator with the will annexed, especially if there is little personal property. 

12. The Names, Relationships, Ages, and Residences of the 

Widow or Widower, and the Heirs, Whether Any of the Heirs are Under a Legal Disability, and, if so, the Names and Residences of their Guardians, so far as Known to the Petitioner, or can with Reasonable Diligence be Ascertained.

Probate Court Rule IV provides that a petition for the probate of a will shall state the name and residence of the widow and heirs-at-law of the deceased, who, if any, are minors or otherwise under disability, and the names and residences of their guardians, if any, in this state, so far as known to the petitioner, or can with reasonable diligence be ascertained. 

43. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15521; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13766.

Probate Court Rule III provides that all petitions to institute any proceedings in the court, when such facts do not already appear of record in the case, shall state whether any person under the age of twenty-one years, or otherwise incapable of prosecuting or defending himself, has any interest in the subject matter thereof, which may be affected by the order, and the name, age and residence of each such minor or other person, when known to the petitioner, and the name of his guardian, if any has been appointed. 

While it is sufficient under Rule IV to name the widow and heirs-at-law, Rule III, in requiring the names of all minors or others under disability having any interest in the subject matter, is sufficiently broad to include all legatees or devisees named in the will, even though they are not heirs-at-law." 

Upon the death of a wife, the widower should be included, because, if the will is refused probate, he will be interested in her estate. The will, of course, presents the names of the beneficiaries. Merely those legatees or devisees under a disability should be reported so that the judge may appoint a guardian ad litem. 45

By statute the court may in its discretion, or, as required by law, appoint some other party than the nominee of the testator as executor. Therefore, the recital in the petition of "or some other suitable person" is appropriate. 99 47 The law provides: "When a will shall have been duly proved and allowed, the probate court shall issue letters testamentary thereon, to the person named the executor therein, if he is legally competent, and shall accept the trust and give bond as required by law, . . . In Breen v. Kehoe," the court said: "This section appears to leave no discretion in the probate judge. If the person named is legally competent and will accept and give the bond, he must be appointed." Nevertheless, the court in approving the appointment of a non-resident alien as executor, said that under this section the probate judge could appoint a non-resident executor in his discretion. 

After citing the provision" which empowers the probate judge to remove an executor residing out of the state, and after citing Gary's statement that this statute prohibited the appointment of a non-resident," the court said: (p. 62) our statute was not intended to absolutely disqualify or prohibit the appointment of non-residents to the office of executor, but to make the non-residence ground for the exercise of a discretion, both in appointment and revocation.” “ 66 ...

When No Executor Named the sole creditor of the estate-was a proper person to be appointed in the place of the executor who had not only removed from the state, but had failed to render an account for more than two years.

In Brown v. Just the testator made provision for an executor in the following language: "I make no selection of an executor of this will, and leave it for the judge of probate, who may be acting when this will becomes operative, to make an appointment of some suitable person for that purpose." 

It was argued that the statute entitled the widow or next of kin to administer with the will annexed. The court found nothing in the statutes of Michigan to abrogate the common law right to delegate the appointment of an executor. 

The statute defines the persons entitled to letters of administration when a man dies intestate. Who is entitled to letters of administration, if a person dies testate, without naming any executor? In sustaining the appointment of an executor by the probate judge in Brown v. Just, the court said: (p. 

681) "The statute (Sec. 15586) applies solely to cases where persons die intestate, or have left wills without any provision for the appointment of executors, and not to cases of delegated authority."

The statute does apply solely to cases of intestacy and those of testacy, wherein no executor is nominated, but in cases of intestacy it is not the exclusive authority for the appointment of an administrator," nor should it be the exclusive authority in cases of testacy. The language of the court in the Brown case should be considered in connection with the rest of the text.

If an executor is named and, for the reason stated in the statute, does not qualify, the court may grant administration with the will annexed to any of the beneficiaries named in the will, if capable, or to such persons as would have been entitled if the testator died intestate." 

56. 118 Mich. 678 (1898). 

57. In re Berner's Estate (1922) 217 Mich. 612. 

58. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15570; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13811.

It is the obvious intention of the legislature to grant administration to those most interested in the estate-the beneficiaries, if desirable, who are not always the next of kin. 

There is no similar statutory provision authorizing the appointment of the beneficiaries as well as the widow, widower, or next of kin, if the testator fails to nominate an executor. 

If the court will modify the intestacy statute to grant administration to the parties most interested in an estate, when a man dies intestate, surely the beneficiaries under a will should be entitled to administration in preference to the persons named in the intestacy statute, if the latter receive nothing under the will.

However, Woerner, in discussing a number of cases and statutes, refers to the Virginia case of Smith v. Lurty," in which a distributee (by virtue of the intestacy statute) was preferred to the principal devisee and legatee under the will. 

The court said: (p. 551) “Not being a distributee, she (the principal devisee) had no preferable claim to be appointed administrator (administratrix w. w. a.), nor preferable right to designate another to be so appointed under the statute." 

Of course, the petitioning distributee in the case happened to be interested in the will. The widow, who had the first right, was not appointed because "she was very hostile to, and not upon speaking terms with, the principal devisee and legatee." 9 60 59. 60.

Inasmuch as the Michigan statute includes the beneficiaries under a will, as a preferred class, if the executor fails to qualify, there seems no valid reason for a different rule if no executor is named in the first instance.

In a Rhode Island case," under a similar statute," the court held that the discretion given the probate judge was not absolute, but a judicial discretion to be exercised in view of the circumstances of the case. The court improperly granted administration of a partially administered estate to an uninterested next of kin upon the death of the executrix. The 

Supreme Court of Probate ordered the appointment of the executor of the executrix's estate, who was entitled to onehalf of the entire unadministered estate by virtue of the executrix's will. The court referred to the next of kin as a mere intermeddler who had no interest whatever in the property to be administered. 61.

The form of petition approved by the judges of probate provides for the address of the nominated administrator, with will annexed or executor. CHAPTER VI 

The Petitions for the Appointment of Guardians for 

Minors, Incompetents, and Spendthrifts

The petitions should be fairly and legibly written in ink or printed and should recite the designation of the court, the character of the paper,' the title of the estate,' and the name and address of the petitioner. 

The petitions should be signed and verified by the petitioners. Upon the cover of the petitions there should be endorsed "State of Michigan, Probate Court for the County of blank," the name of the estate, the character of the petition, and the name of the attorney, if any, appearing for the petitioner.*

The petition for the appointment of a guardian for a minor should recite: 

1. The interest of the petitioner and his right to apply to the court; 

2. The name and age of the minor; 

3. The domicile of the minor and the address and name of the person with whom the minor is living at the time the petition is filed; 

4. The names and addresses of the father and mother; if either or both are dead, such fact should be alleged together with the order in which they died; 

1. Probate Court Rule I requires a written petition. The rules do not require a designation of the court, as in the case of law or chancery proceedings-See Michigan Court Rule 7-though it is an excellent practice. 2. 

Rule I requires an endorsement upon the petition of the character of the paper and the title of the matter (or estate). The word "endorsement" undoubtedly refers to the cover of the petition, nevertheless it is proper that these facts appear on the face of the petition. 3.

Probate Court Rule III. 

4. Probate Court Rule I. 78

5. If both parents are dead, the names, ages, relationships and residences of the next of kin of the minor; 

6. Whether the minor has property within the state of 

Michigan and the county or counties where located; 

7. The estimated value of the real and personal estate ; 

8. That no general guardian has been appointed in Michigan; 

9. That it is necessary that a guardian be appointed to have the custody of the minor and/or that a guardian is necessary for the protection and care of the estate of the minor; 

10. A prayer that the nominee of the petitioner or some other suitable person be appointed guardian; 

11. The address of the nominated guardian. If the nominee is not the petitioner, a consent to act upon the part of the nominee should be endorsed upon the petition. 

Explanation of the Recitals in the Petition for the 

Appointment of a Guardian for a Minor 

1. The Interest of the Petitioner and His Right to Apply to the Court.

Probate Court Rule III provides that all petitions, except nominations of guardians by minors, should state the facts with common certainty which show the interest of the petitioner and his right to apply to the court. 5. 6. or 

The statute prescribes the persons entitled to apply: "Petitions for the appointment of guardians may be made by any minor over fourteen years of age, in his own behalf, by the father, mother, or next of kin of any person, by any other person whom the judge of probate, upon examination into the facts and circumstances of any particular case, shall determine to be a proper person to make such petition.' 995 In Burrows v.

Right of Parents fourteen years of age could appear personally before the probate court and orally nominate a guardian. While Probate 

Court Rule I provides that all petitions should be in writing, it is doubtful if the Supreme Court intended to deprive an infant of the privilege of an oral petition. Rule III in requiring a jurisdictional showing as well as a verification by the petitioner, exempts minors. If an oral petition is made by a minor, it is good practice to request his signature upon a written application in order to make more complete the official record of the appointment. It is a simple matter to complete a written application at the hearing. The suggestion should not be a cause for delay in an appointment upon an oral petition. 

If a minor, being above the age of fourteen years, resides more than ten miles from the place of holding court, "his nomination of a guardian, made in writing, and signed by himself, may be certified to the judge of probate by a justice of the peace, or by the township clerk of the township in which such minor resides or by a notary public, which shall have the same effect as if made in the presence of the judge: 

Provided, That if such minor shall be temporarily away from this state, in any other state or territory of the United States, such nomination may be certified with like effect, by any civil or military officer of the United States, in such state or territory, holding a commission from the President of the 

United States, or the Governor of this State." 

Undoubtedly the court would heed a certification by a

United States diplomatic or consular official, if the minor was temporarily in a foreign country.

If competent, the father or mother of a minor, and if one of them is dead, the survivor, are the only persons entitled by relationship or any other reason to the guardianship of minor children. (See discussion under topics 9 and 10.) If any 7. 8. 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15767; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13954, amended by P. A. (1917) No. 60.

other person makes application, he must allege his reasons for petitioning. If some one other than one of the parents is nominated for guardian, an allegation must be made concerning the unfitness of the child's parents.

Since the power of appointing guardians is to be exercised for the benefit of the ward rather than the applicant, the class of persons entitled to petition under the statute is only limited by the discretion of the probate judge, after learning the circumstances. A friend or neighbor may be better qualified to testify concerning the condition of a minor than an aunt or uncle. Of course, if an application is presented by anyone else than the parents, the latter must be served with notice. (See chapter on notice.) 

The statute provides that "In case of the death of either father or mother, the surviving parent may, by last will in writing, appoint a guardian or guardians However, if the surviving parent was not a resident of Michigan, and the custody of the child was in the lawful control of a Michigan citizen for one year or upwards prior to the parent's death, the guardianship can not be changed by will." 10. 11.

The statute empowers the probate judge to appoint guardians to minors who are inhabitants or residents in his county." 

The words "inhabitant" and "resident," as employed by the legislature in this connection are synonymous with the case law term "domicile." The domicile of an infant follows that of his father." Physical presence, however, is not a necessity. A minor can not change his domicile, unless possibly after emancipation. If a father abandons his family, the child's domicile may remain or may follow that of the mother, provided the child accompanies the mother. In re Stockman (1888) 71 Mich. 180, the court said: (p. 193) "Comity cannot be considered in a case like this, when the future welfare of the child is the vital question in the case.

authorities are divided as to whether marriage entitles a minor to establish a new domicile. In case of divorce, the domicile follows that of the parent given custody. If both have an equal right to custody, the child's domicile would be that of the parent with whom he makes his home. If both parents are dead, some authorities recognize the grandparents as natural guardians with the power to change the child's domicile. A domiciliary guardian may change the domicile within the state, if the child lives with the guardian. An illegitimate child takes the domicile of the mother. 

4. The Names and Addresses of the Father and Mother; if Either or Both are Dead, Such Fact Should be Alleged Together with the Order in Which They Died. 

Since the father or mother are primarily entitled to guardianship, if competent; their names and addresses should appear upon the petition in order that the court may direct a notice to them of the pending application."

The domicile of the parents generally controls that of the minor. Possibly the alleged domicile of the infant may prove to be inaccurate after the taking of proof concerning the domicile of the parents. If both parents are dead, the domicile of the infant may be that of the last survivor." 16. 17.
