The statute authorizes the appointment of guardians to manage the Michigan property of minors living beyond the state."

In the case of In re William Rice, the court said that "our laws contemplate that guardians may be appointed for infants, whatever may be their residence, who have property in this state, and do not recognize any absolute right in foreign guardians to be recognized. Whatever respect is paid them springs from comity and not from law, and is therefore not a matter of right." The value of a minor's property is a considerable factor in the selection of a guardian, unless it be one of the parents in which case less weight should be given to the value—the relationship being far more important. 

8. That No General Guardian Has Been Appointed in Michigan.

The statute provides that "When minors, incompetents or other persons shall have had a guardian duly appointed by the probate court of any county in this state, and such ward shall have become a resident of and have obtained a legal domicile in another county, a guardian may be appointed over said ward by the probate court of the county of such domicile : 

Provided, That such guardian shall not be appointed without the consent of the probate court which appointed the previous guardian. ,, 20

Upon the hearing for the appointment of a guardian, either for the person or estate or both, a showing of necessity is of vital importance, especially when the petitioner is not one of the parents and the latter are alive. It would serve no good purpose to require a recital of the facts showing necessity in the petition. It is sufficient if it merely be alleged that a guardian is necessary. 23. ,, 22

The statute provides that "The father or mother of the minor, and if one of them be deceased, then the survivor thereof, being respectively competent to transact their own business, and otherwise suitable, shall be entitled to the custody of the person of the minor and to the care of his education.” 

While the statute apparently limits the right of the parents to custody alone, it is well settled that they are entitled to the guardianship of the estate of their children, unless unfit." 

In some instances a widow or widower may be competent for the custody of the minor children and nevertheless quite incompetent for the management of a large and complicated estate.** If a widow or widower is unfamiliar with business practice the court might well place the management of the minor's property in the hands of an experienced person. 24 In case of a divorce the father is entitled to the custody of 24.

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15768; C. L. Sec. 13955, amended by P. A. (1921) No. 344. "At common law the father was entitled to the custody of his minor children. It has been quite a struggle for the courts, even with the aid of statutes, to avoid following this rule. In the early cases, where the father was leading a life of open profligacy, he was given the custody of the children. Gradually, considering the statutes and the discretionary power of the court, it has come to be the rule that the welfare of the infant is the chief consideration in controversies of this kind, and courts will give the custody of the child to either father or mother, and sometimes will deny to both of them the custody of their children." 41 L. R. A. (N. S.) 564. "Right of Parent to Appointment as Guardian of a Minor Child." 

33 L. R. A. (N. S.) 868. 

Lawrence v. Thomas (1892) 84 Iowa 362, 365.

Relatives Have No Preferred Right the children twelve years or over and the mother to the children below the age of twelve. The court may disregard this arrangement if the best interests of the children so dictate.25 

The relatives have no preference in the appointment of guardians." Many times a neighbor is better qualified than a relative who happens to live in another city. Obviously a relative should be preferred, if at all qualified." 

In the case of In re Stockman the court said: (p. 191) "Who shall or may be appointed guardian is within the discretion of the court. Relatives of the infant are usually selected, and those nearest of kin are usually preferred when otherwise competent, and as between those entitled, the question to be determined in making the selection is, and always should be, what will be for the best interest of the ward under all the circumstances. It should control everything else.""

The residence of the guardian may properly influence the court in making or denying an appointment. If appointed, the address should be on file for convenience. An endorsement of consent will in many cases save considerable time and expense.

Explanation of the Recitals in the Petition 1. The Interest of the Petitioner and His Right to Apply to the Court.

Probate Court Rule III provides that all petitions, except nominations of guardians by minors, should state the facts with common certainty which show the interest of the petitioner and his right to apply to the court. 

In 1871 the relatives or friends of an incompetent person were authorized to petition for guardianship.' In 1897 the county directors of the poor could petition, if the incompetent 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1871) Sec. 4822. 

Who May Petition

was a public charge, as well as the relatives or friends." 

Guardians for spendthrifts were appointed upon the application of any superintendent of the poor of the county, or directors of the poor, or justice of the peace of the township of which such spendthrift was an inhabitant. The statute now prescribes that: "Petitions for the appointment of guardians may be made. . . by the father, mother or next of kin of any person, by the superintendent of the poor, or either of them, of any county, by the directors of the poor of any city, the supervisor of any township or ward, or by any other person whom the judge of probate, upon examination into the facts and circumstances of any particular case, shall determine to be a proper person to make such petition." 994 More latitude is given as to who may petition in guardianship proceedings than in the administration of an estate of a deceased person because the interest of the ward is the controlling factor rather than the interest of the petitioner. 

Provision is made for personal service upon the respondent and the next of kin. (See chapter on notice, p. 104.)

The statute empowers the probate judge to appoint guardians to mental incompetents or spendthrifts who are inhabitants or residents in his county. 8. That it is Necessary that a Guardian be Appointed.

The cause of mental incompetency need not be alleged in the petition." In Norton v. Sherman a petition was held sufficient which recited that the respondent was old, feeble and infirm and that he was mentally incompetent to have the charge and management of his property and had been mentally incompetent for some time." In Partello v. Holton an appointment was held void in a collateral attack because the petition alleged that the respondent was unable to provide suitable and comfortable maintenance for his family and that he made foolish bargains and was unable to manage his personal affairs. In holding that the allegations did not show 

11. 68 Mich. 348 (1888). 

12. In re Miller's Estate (1912) 173 Mich. 467. 13. 

58 Mich. 549 (1885).

" 14 mental incompetency under the statute, the court said: (p. 

379) "The power of appointment of a guardian by the probate court is derived from the statute, and, in order to obtain jurisdiction in such cases, the statute must be strictly construed.' In the case of In re Brown the petition set forth that the respondent was incompetent to have the care, charge and management of his property and that he was old and infirm. It also was alleged that he was so addicted to the excessive use of intoxicating drinks as to be liable to become the victim of designing persons, and that through intoxication and foolish speculation, he wasted more than half of a considerable estate within fifteen years. The court said that the last of these allegations would have been proper if he had been considered a spendthrift rather than a mental incompetent. (p. 328) "The statute contemplates the existence of insanity or mental infirmity that is equivalent in destroying mental incompetency. The incompetency set out in the petition is not necessarily mental incompetency. Age and infirmity are not inconsistent with vigor of intellect; and, therefore, the averment that respondent is old and infirm can not stand as a substitute for a direct averment of mental incompetency.'

In the case of In re Storick the petition was defective and the court said: (p. 689) "Unless the petition either follows the words of the statute, or uses language and states facts fully equivalent, it cannot give jurisdiction. This is a class of cases in which the citizen is sought to be deprived of both liberty and property, and those who seek to accomplish such a result must do so upon statements that, if true, leave no doubt that the case falls within the statute.' 15 " 16 14. 15. 16.

9. That No General Guardian Has Been Appointed in Michigan.

The statute provides that "When minors, incompetents or other persons shall have had a guardian duly appointed by the probate court of any county in this state, and such ward shall have become a resident of and have obtained a legal domicile in another county, a guardian may be appointed over said ward by the probate court of the county of such domicile :

10. A Prayer that the Nominee of the Petitioner or Some 

Other Suitable Person be Appointed Guardian.

Woerner says: "An old English rule discriminated against the heir-at-law, or the one next entitled to the lunatic's real estate after his death, as custodian of the lunatic's person. 

This maxim was severely criticized, as not founded on reason, and prevailing only in the barbarous times before the nation was civilized. A similar objection existed, also, though to a far more limited extent, against the next of kin to the lunatic. 

Both these rules are now disregarded in England as well as in the United States; on the contrary, 'the law now supposes 

In the case of In re Bassett (1888) 68 Mich. 348 the court said under the then existing law (p. 354) "It is clear that the judge of probate had no authority to appoint a guardian over the person (of an incompetent), and not the estate; neither could be appoint one over his estate, and not over his person." 

27. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15799; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13986. 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15791; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13978. 28.

that those who stand nearest to the lunatic by the ties of kindred, will treat him with more affection and patient fortitude than strangers in blood." 29 "It is not a matter of course to commit the guardianship of the estate of a lunatic to those who are presumptively entitled to it upon his death, as his heirs or next of kin; but they will be appointed if it satisfactorily appears that they are the persons most likely to protect the property from loss. "The governing principle on which courts act in appointing a guardian is the interest of the lunatic himself, and not that of those who may have the right of succession, or the next of kin. "Love, ties of blood, affinities of relationship, similarity of habit, taste, and association ought to be weighed and considered in determining who is to be the guardian. Although it is unusual not to appoint the party nominated by the person suing out the commission, yet the court has full power to appoint, in its discretion, any suitable person 99 80

CHAPTER VII Special Guardian 

The Statute provides: "The judge of probate of any county, upon a proper showing, upon such notice as he shall direct, pending any application for the appointment of a general guardian as aforesaid, or pending any appeal or litigation in relation to the appointment of such general guardian, may, if he shall deem it fit and proper, under the circumstances of the case, appoint a special guardian of such person. Such special guardian shall in proper cases and when so ordered by the court, have the care and custody of the person of his ward and the management of all his estate, and shall give the security specified in the next section.' He shall hold his office until the question of appointment of a general guardian be decided, or until he shall be discharged by the judge of probate.' 99 2 The appointment of a special guardian, during an appeal from the appointment of a general guardian is invalid if the statute has not been followed in appointing the general guardian. In re Guardianship of Bassett the petition for the appointment of a general guardian for an alleged mental incompetent failed to state the fact of marriage or give the name of the incompetent's wife. The court said: "The petition should show all persons who will be affected by the action sought to be taken; and this would manifestly include the presumptive heirs-at-law and distributees." 

The order of appointment of the general guardian was defective in not appointing a guardian of the estate. The judge "had no authority to appoint a guardian over the person, and not of the estate; neither could he appoint one over 

1. Bond of Guardian, Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15775; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13962.

68 Mich. 348 (1888). 96

97 his estate, and not over his person.' The order was also defective in not adjudging the ward to be a mental incompetent. 5 39 4 The "question of appointing a special guardian is addressed to the sound discretion of the court and is determined by the apparent condition of the ward, or of his estate, or both. It is an auxiliary proceeding and it is apparent that a proper showing, within the meaning of the statute, may be made by the proofs produced at the hearing upon the original application." 997 9.

The jurisdiction for the appointment of a special guardian Relationship does not depend upon the relationship of the petitioner with the alleged mental incompetent.* 

Discretion of Court

The statute provides that the judge of probate may appoint Notice a special guardian upon such notice as he shall direct. In 

Devereaux v. Janes' an order appointing a special guardian was entered upon the same day the petition was filed. The court said: (p. 267) “The order was made on the same day that the petition was filed, and, more than this, the order recites that the order was made 'on reading and filing the petition.' This language fairly imports that simultaneously with the filing of the paper which put the court in motion the order appointing a special guardian was made, thus negativing any presumption of notice which might otherwise arise."

CHAPTER VIII Notice 

Probate proceedings are defined by Justice Campbell as "statutory prerogative proceedings to determine the status of a dead man's estate, and in no sense to be regarded as contentious litigation inter partes." Sanford, J., in Wilson v. 

Hartford Fire Ins. Co.,' states, "A proceeding in a probate court to administer upon the estate of a deceased person is a proceeding in rem, not in personam. The property within the jurisdiction of the court is the defendant, the executor or administrator is its representative, and all claiming any interest in that property under the deceased are parties to the proceeding." This statement has been frequently cited, but not so frequently followed to its logical conclusion. Mr. 

Justice Holmes has said of actions in rem," " a proceeding in rem, in the proper sense of the words, might give a clear title without other notice than a seizure of the res and an exhibition of the warrant to those in charge. (citing 2 Browne, Civil Law, 398) The general requirement of advertisement in admiralty cases is said to be due to rules of court." The language of some cases, indeed, have followed the "in rem" theory literally, but by far the greater number have imposed limitations upon the probate power by requiring notice, either actual or constructive, of every action taken by the court which might affect persons claiming an interest in the estate." .. · 4 1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 1 

People ex. rel. Frazer v. Wayne Circuit Judge (1878) 39 Mich. 198. 

164 Fed. 817, 19 LRA (NS) 553, (C. C. A., 8th Circ. 1908), p. 819 of the Federal Reporter. 

Tyler v. Judges, (1900) 175 Mass. 71, 51 LRA 433. 

As, Barlow's Estate, (1922) 152 Minn. 249. 

In Caulk v. Lowe, (1919) 74 Okla. 191, 178 Pac. 101, the decedent's non-resident daughter was not mentioned in the petition for probate, and no notice was mailed to her as required by statute. 

Three and a half years after the administrator had been discharged, this daughter succeeded in having the proceeding set aside, the court saying: "That a judgment rendered without 98

NOTICE In most of the states, as in Michigan, the legislatures have enacted statutes requiring notice in some form to the interested parties, and courts have not infrequently held these statutory requirements to be jurisdictional.' And while a strict following of the Civil theory of actions in rem would dispense with the necessity of notice (to all but persons who have regularly appeared in the particular proceeding) both legislative enactment and judicial modification have resulted in erecting requirements of notice as to practically all incidental steps necessary in the administration of an estate, and have even created different rules and precedents governing these various different steps and proceedings. 6.

The necessity of notice is most frequently discussed in admitting wills to probate, and in appointing administrators. 

Statutes providing for some form of notice are found in practically every jurisdiction. In Michigan, the Legislature has provided for the manner of giving notice in probate court, by personal service fourteen days before the hearing, or publication once a week for three successive weeks, and has required the giving of such notice when application is made for the appointment of an administrator, for letters of administration with the will annexed, for administration de bonis non, and for the probate of a will." If an heir is a resident of a foreign country, service on the consul of such foreign nation, sixty days in advance, is required." And it 11.

No Uniformity 

Among States has been held that notice in conformity with these statutory provisions is necessary to the court's jurisdiction." 

Notice may be waived, if in writing and acknowledged, by any legally competent person; and by the guardian or testimentary trustee of a minor or mentally incompetent person, except as to notice of hearing of petitions or reports made by the guardian as such."

In Gillett v. Needham," the affidavit of publication was defective, and the court, through Cooley, J., in holding the probate court to have been without jurisdiction to enter the order of administration said: "To authorize the making of such an order however, it must have been shown to the court that the notice to parties concerned, which the statute requires, had previously been published; for while the court is one of general probate jurisdiction, it cannot take cognizance of particular cases until in some legal manner the parties are before it." In other words, the court is not interested solely in the res, but now demands that the interested parties be brought into court. And in Kammerer v. Morlock," the probate court was held to have been without jurisdiction to appoint an administrator on Monday, August 5, when the notice of hearing stated that it would be held on Monday, August 9. 

The statutes of each state, containing different provisions as to notice, uniformity among the cases is not to be anticipated, and decisions from other states can be expected to provide no more than an analogy, with, perhaps, an appropriate discussion." And while the courts continue to act upon the "in rem" theory in determining the status of the "res," they also seem to find it desirable to adjudicate the rights of the persons claiming an interest in the estate. The American sense of fair play suggests that before the heirs, or creditors, be precluded from asserting their rights in the estate, they should be notified of the proceeding." Actual notice being often impracticable, and the administration being, after all, the more important purpose, constructive notice is allowed. If a determination of heirs is desired, publication in accordance with Sec. 15538 is sufficient," and no provision is made for personal service of any kind. And notice of the appointment of a special administrator is not required." She had not been served with notice, but knew of the proceeding and was present in the court room at the time of hearing. Nevertheless, the Probate Judge was held to have been without jurisdiction to act. "If Mrs. North was mentally 'incompetent to have the care and control of her person and the management of her property, she was incompetent to give consent to any legal proceedings, or to waive any of her legal rights relating thereto; and if she was competent, the probate court had no power to appoint a guardian for her or her estate, even though all her relatives and friends should request it, and she desire and consent to it.' The court further stated: "The probate court derives its jurisdiction to appoint guardians for insane and incompetent persons entirely from the statute, and in order to obtain jurisdiction in such cases the provisions of the statute must be strictly pursued." That the liberty of the respondent, a living person, is at stake, perhaps explains why this feature of the probate court's jurisdiction is, seemingly, considered to be purely in personam. In Probate Judge v. Abbott the executor, who was the residuary legatee, gave a bond conditioned upon the payment of all the debts and legacies. In Hatheway v. Weeks (1876) 34 Mich. 237 the court said: (p. 245) he (the executor) is bound by the conditions (of the bond) 'to pay all the debts and legacies of the testator' not conditionally, in case there were found sufficient assets, but absolutely. The giving of the bond conclusively admits assets."

the executor, as residuary legatee, has given bond to pay debts and legacies, or at least that they may be, as in other cases; and second, that when the judge of probate proposes himself to hear and pass upon claims, notice by publication is an admissible process for bringing the parties concerned before. him." 11. 12. 13. 66 In delivering the court's opinion, Justice Cooley stated (citing Alger v. Colwell") that a hearing upon claims was unnecessary, if an executor, as residuary legatee, had given a bond to pay all debts and legacies. However, he suggested that it might be proper to hear a claim, if the executor and claimant failed to agree." In the event the claim was heard by the court, the usual notice by publication would not answer the requirements of law. He said: (p. 285) . . . the case is wholly different when an executor, who is also residuary legatee, has given bond to pay all claims. There is no longer a proceeding in rem, for the res disappears when the estate passes from the control of the probate court and becomes merged in the individual estate of the executor himself. What before was a jus ad rem in the creditor, to be enforced by the aid of the probate court as a lien upon an estate in its charge, becomes now a personal obligation of the executor and his sureties, attaching itself to no specific property," and concerning no other persons whatsoever. (p. 286) The res having disappeared, and the demand having become the personal obligation of the executor and his sureties, they are entitled to have it treated as a personal obligation in the proceedings to prove it. They are entitled to their day in court, and to personal service of process, or a substitute therefor, to bring them before the court." The allowance of the claim by the judge was held to be ineffectual. 

2 Gray (Mass.) 404 (1854). 

See Lafferty v. People's Saving Bank (1889) 76 Mich. 35, 58. 

See, however, the majority opinion in Lafferty v. People's 

Saving Bank (1889) 76 Mich. 35, holding that the bond of the residuary legatee does not release the property from the creditor's liens, but merely gives an additional remedy. See statement on p. 58 concerning the penal sum of a residuary bond.

Discretion of 

Judge in Hearing Claims 

Requirements if Commission Appointed

At the time letters testamentary or of administration are granted the judge of probate may determine to hear the claims himself" or appoint one or more commissioners for that purpose. Of course, the letters of administration are not issued until the appointee qualifies by filing the usual administration bond. A petition, requesting that the judge hear the claims, is unnecessary." If the judge fails to act, a petition would be proper, by either a creditor or the administrator. 

Oftentimes a creditor or administrator may advance an excellent reason for one method of hearing claims as opposed to the other-based possibly on the nature of the claim. Again a petition would be desirable.

If the judge elects to appoint commissioners on claims, he should designate in the commission a time limit within which creditors may present their claims; not to exceed one year nor be less than four months." He should also designate in the

And further on in the opinion (p. 18) “This action of the probate judge (the heari of claims either by a commission or himself) may be had upon his own motion, or upon the application of any claimant or of the executor; and it is not perceived why it was not as much a neglect of duty in the plaintiff (the creditor) not to make this application (for a hearing) as it was of the executor."

The administrator may have little or no unadministered property left for a delayed creditor."

If the notice has in fact been given, the commissioners may proceed without the proof of such publication before them." 

Before holding their first meeting, the commissioners should swear to discharge their duties faithfully."

The commissioners should select any convenient time during the day for their meetings. Generally two meetings are scheduled.

There is no fixed rule requiring the lapse of any particular period of time between the first and last meeting. No final action should be taken upon a claim presented at the first meeting, as objections can be made at the last meeting. Experience has demonstrated that the claimant may believe the matter concluded at the first meeting and not attend the last meeting. With a long interval of time between the meetings the recollections of the commissioners concerning the evidence presented is often meager.

Claims, however, may be presented at any time during the allowed limit, as fixed by the judge. While all of the commissioners should hear the presentation, it would seem to be not improper for a single commissioner to take the testimony. 

However, if two are appointed, both must agree in order to allow a claim. 

24. In Brown v. Forsche (1880) 43 Mich. 492 Justice Cooley, in delivering the opinion of the court said: (p. 500) "For all purposes except the final passing of his accounts, we think the administrator is to be considered as having settled the estate, when having paid the debts reported by the commissioners, he has handed over the remaining property to the distributees." (citing Calkins v. Smith (1879) 41 Mich. 409.) "The distributees are then entitled to the property, and the administrator fully administers when he allows them to take it in the proper proportion. The claimant, who unexpectedly appears afterwards, must seek his remedy against the estate in the condition in which he then finds it. The misfortune of finding it administered is his, and not that of the administrator." 25. 26.

In Smith v. Lloyd Estate (1889) 76 Mich. 619 the court held that a creditor waived his right to ask for a new commission by appealing from the disagreement of the first commission. 

In Smith v. Lloyd Estate (1889) 76 Mich. 619 the court said: (pp. 621, 622) “A disagreement by equal division of the members of a tribunal of trial (commission on claims) is according to universal authority, equivalent to a denial of the claim in litigation, and has the force of a judgment. Until the statute of 1883, it was the final action of disagreeing commissioners, and the claimant had no other remedy except by appeal. In 1883 a law was passed (P. A. 1883, p. 100) which provided for the appointment by the probate court of a third commissioner to sit upon such claims as were not agreed upon. While the statute says that the court of probate 'shall appoint,' it cannot be assumed that the judge has any right to do this on his own motion, and without the application of some party interested. It is an important matter and requires consideration of capacity, fitness, and impartiality. It is a proceeding directly affecting both the claimant and the estate, and both have a right to be heard. The choice of commissioners is not a matter in which the parties have no voice. It is one which, especially where it is made to furnish an umpire, should never be made without giving opportunity to be heard. Notice in such a case is as much an implied condition as it is in all other analagous cases of action affecting private rights." 

See same case in Durand v. Circuit Judge (1889) 76 Mich. 624.

34 There is no formal procedure regulating the manner in which claims must be presented." The commission is considered as a special tribunal," though not a court," and its action is not under the control of the probate court." Any one of the commissioners "shall be authorized to administer oaths" when required or proper in the investigation and trial of questions."

The commission may adjourn its meetings during the time fixed by the judge for presenting claims, but it can not receive for consideration any claim not presented before the time limit expires." After the time limit expires they may complete the hearings on all claims previously presented."

Substance is more regarded than form in such cases, and the simpler the statement the better, if sufficient to apprise the court and parties interested of the real claim."

In Gilbert v. Taylor's Estate (1927) 237 Mich. 225 the probate judge heard the claims himself and because he was notified that the claims could not be presented at the scheduled meeting on the last day of the time allowed creditors, he entered an order adjourning the meeting one week. It was objected that the order was void by virtue of (section 15687), which bars creditors who fail to present their claims within the time allowed. The court in answering the objection, said that (section 15680) should not be overlooked and cited with approval the statement in Seilnacht v. Wayne Probate Judge, (1918) 201 Mich. 536, to the effect that a creditor could present his claim as a matter of right at any time before the estate was closed. (Section 15679) was also cited, which allows the judge to extend the time for the allowance of claims. It was held that the probate court had authority to grant the adjournment. 32. 33. 34. 35. 36. 37.

"If any person shall be liable as security for the deceased or have any other claim against his estate which cannot be proved as a debt before the commissioners, or allowed by them, the same may be presented with the proper proof to the probate court, or to the commissioners, who shall state the same in their report, if such claim shall be presented to them; and in case such claim shall be made against such estate by reason of any bond or agreement of any kind in writing, signed by the deceased and binding him to pay any certain sum or sums for the support of the claimant for the lifetime of such claimant or for a term of years, or to perform certain work for another, for the non-performance of which his estate is liable, or binding deceased to pay any sums as rental during a lease, the administrator of said estate, by and with the consent of such claimant and the approval of the judge of probate, may compromise and settle such claim in such manner as shall be just and shall be determined by said judge of probate or probate court, and the same so determined shall be paid as other debts of said deceased, and the administrator in effecting such compromise is empowered, if necessary and if so ordered by the judge of probate, after such notice as he shall direct, to purchase the real estate covered by the lease and in case of the purchase of the real estate as above provided the same shall be treated as personal property in the hands of the executor or administrator and disposed of and distributed as such.' 99 1 The commission is not authorized to pass upon the validity of contingent claims, it acts ministerially in hearing and reporting the proof of the claims to the probate court.' "If the court shall be satisfied from the report of the commissioners, or by the proof exhibited, said court may order 

1. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15708; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13897. 

2. Buchoz v. Pray (1877) 36 Mich. 429. 118

CONTINGENT CLAIMS 

119 the executor or administrator to retain in his hands sufficient estate to pay such contingent claim when the same shall become absolute, or, if the estate shall be insolvent, sufficient to pay a proportion equal to the dividends of the other credi- 198 tors.'

It seems unjust and a cause of possible hardship to the heirs and creditors if assets are to be retained for every contingent claim regardless of merit. It is clear that the legislature intended an exercise of discretion by the court. Because of the uncertainty that often exists as to whether an estate will be solvent or insolvent before administration is closed, it would appear to be practically incumbent upon the court to order or deny a retention of assets in order to be fair toward the absolute and contingent creditors. The proportion that should be set aside should be definitely fixed by the court.* 

In allowing proof of contingent claims, it appears to be the intent of the legislature that the exact present and future indebtedness of an estate should be definitely determined at the end of the period allowed creditors for the presentation of claims. 99 8 "If such contingent claim shall become absolute, and shall be presented to the probate court, or to the executor or administrator, at any time within two years from the time limited for other creditors to present their claims to the commissioners, it may be allowed by the probate court upon due proof, or it may be proved before the commissioners already appointed, or before others to be appointed for that purpose, in the same manner as if presented for allowance before the commissioners had made their report, and the persons interested shall have the same right of appeal as in other cases. 

3. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15709; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13898. 4.

It is practically impossible to estimate the monetary liability that may arise when certain contingent claims become absolute. 

Probably a compromise will offer the only solution in many cases.

"If the claim of any person shall accrue or become absolute, at any time after the time limited for creditors to present their claims, (this undoubtedly refers to any time after the two year period following the time allowed creditors for presenting claims, because provision for allowance is made for all claims becoming absolute prior to the termination of the two year period) the person having such claim may present it to the probate court, and prove the same at any time within one year after it shall accrue or become absolute, and if established in the manner provided in this chapter, the executor or administrator shall be required to pay it, if he shall have sufficient assets for that purpose, and shall be required to pay such part as he shall have assets to pay; and if real or personal estate shall afterwards come to his possession, he shall be required to pay such claim, or such part as he may have assets sufficient to pay, not exceeding the proportion of the other creditors, in such time as the probate court may prescribe."'

The owner of a contingent claim may protect himself by a presentation during the allotted time, while the claim is still contingent. In Verdier v. Roach (1892) 96 Cal. 467, the court said: (p. 

479) "... it may be remarked that if contingent claims are not to be entirely ignored in the administration of estates, it is quite as important that they should be presented for allowance, as that absolute claims should be presented. Otherwise, how is any provision whatever to be made for them before the estate is settled? Timely notice of all claims which may prejudicially affect an estate should be given to the administrator, so that he may have an opportunity to investigate their merits, and to contest them, if advisable, before the evidence of their invalidity shall be lost. That they should be forever barred if not presented within the time prescribed by law is surely no greater hardship than that absolute claims should be so barred."

In Mann v. Everts" an administrator defaulted after having consummated a sale of land in the probate court. After closing the estate of one of the sureties on the administrator's sale bond, suit was brought against his heirs. It was contended "that the county courts are the sole tribunals for the adjustment of all claims against the estates of deceased persons, whether such claims are absolute or contingent; and that where administration has been had upon an estate, a notice of opportunity given for creditors to present their claims for allowance, they must do so within the time limited, or their claims will be barred." The court said: (p. 377) "It seems to us obvious from the language of these provisions (substantially identical with sections 15710, 15712 and 15713 of the 1929 Michigan Compiled Laws) that the contingent claim referred to is one which accrues and becomes absolute before the administration of the estate is closed, and before a decree of distribution of the estate has been made by the county court. We do not think they were intended to or do apply to a contingent claim like the present, which did not accrue and which was incapable of being established by proof until years after the estate of (deceased) had been fully administered. "The breach of the bond did not occur until nearly ten years after this time. The very existence of any future liability of that estate was uncertain, depending upon the contingency. If he should faithfully perform that condition, the estate would have to answer for no breach. But if the administrator failed to perform, what damages would result from the breach, and how could they be ascertained? Suppose that breach were one which would furnish a claim for nominal damages only, or for a portion of the penal sum. Is it not 13.

In the case of In re Thayer's Estate (1920) 210 Mich. 696 it was held that an estate could not be reopened to allow a contingent claim that had become absolute. An estate is absolved of all liability once closed.

According to our view, the statute refers to a contingent claim or liability which can be established by proof and the amount ascertained. This bond was an obligation of a peculiar character given by the administrator to the circuit judge as security, for the faithful performance of a trust or legal duty. Such an obligation cannot be considered as a debt, either in praesenti or in futuro. It may, in case of a breach, be the foundation of a debt or liability by a claim arising from it in the nature of a debt, or arise from the breach and not from the mere existence of the obligation.' 22 15 can

18 10 lating actions of creditors against heirs, devisees, next of kin, and legatees." They are not barred by section 14 of chapter 

53, because they are not proper to be allowed by the commissioners; and the provisions of sections 43, 44, 46, 47 and 48* are not for the purpose of presenting them as claims, but to give the holders of contingent claims an opportunity, within a limited time and on prescribed conditions, to secure payment of the claims through administration, instead of resorting to those to whom the remainder of the estate may have been assigned."

The statutory provisions allowing suits against heirs by creditors were substantially the same in Wisconsin and Minnesota at the date each case was decided." Recovery was allowed if a creditor had been unsuccessful in obtaining satisfaction after proper proceedings in the probate court. Proper proceedings could not be had in the probate court upon contingent claims maturing after administration was closed. 

The Michigan statutes are considerably restricted as compared to those in Minnesota and Wisconsin. "When the heirs, devisees or legatees shall have received real or personal estate, and shall be liable for any debts as mentioned in this chapter, they shall be liable in proportion to the estate they may have respectively received; and the creditor may have any proper action at suit or in law or equity, and shall have a right to recover his claim against a part or all of such heirs, devisees or legatees, to the amount of the estate they may have respectively received, but no such action shall be maintained unless commenced within one year from the time the claim be allowed or established."" ... shall be liable for . . . debts as mentioned in this chapter." The provision authorizing a suit only covers debts which are mentioned in the chapter.

In Rankin v. City of Big Rapids" a contingent claim not presented for allowance during administration was allowed to be maintained in equity against a distributee of an estate. 

While decided in the Federal court, Michigan law governed the decision.

The court followed the Wisconsin and Minnesota construction. The court said: (p. 675) "It is evident that the purpose of these provisions was to hold the estate open for a limited time to enable a creditor having a contingent claim, which might become absolute within that period, to come in and share with other creditors before administration should be closed; and, if it did not become absolute, the estate should be closed without further regard to such claim.” 

The jurisdiction of equity was relied upon in allowing recovery rather than the statute," authorizing actions against the heirs, as done in Wisconsin and Minnesota. The court said: (p. 680) "We think the case of Allen v. Conklin" must be regarded as refuting the contention that the probate laws afford the only remedy a creditor may have in Michigan for the recovery of a claim against the estate of a deceased person, and as establishing the proposition that, when those laws do not avail to protect equities arising from special circumstances, its equity courts will give relief by applying the principles and remedies peculiar to that jurisdiction. And such is the course of decisions in other states in reference to their pro-

If the legislature intended that proof of all contingent claims, regardless of maturity, should be presented to the probate court or commissioners on claims, and that assets should be retained, possibly the jurisdiction of equity may supplement that of the probate court in another direction. In Verdier v. Roach the court said: (p. 478) "Counsel... have suggested difficulties in the way of providing for the payment or security of that class of contingent claims, the amounts of which cannot be ascertained within the ordinary period of administration. Such difficulties have always been encountered, but generally, if not always, overcome. (See 2 Williams on Executors, 6th Am. ed., 1447-1453.) Where the limited jurisdiction of a distinct probate court has proved to be inadequate, the original jurisdiction of courts of equity over the administration of estates has been invoked." 99 25

CHAPTER XI 

Sales

A petition to sell real property of a deceased person to comply with the statute must set forth: 

1. That the personal estate is insufficient to pay the debts of the deceased and the charges of administering his estate; or¹ 

2. That it is for the best interest of all persons interested in the estate that some or all of the realty be sold, for the purpose of paying debts or charges, instead of disposing of personalty; or 

3. That such a sale is necessary to preserve or prevent a sacrifice of the estate, or to carry out the provisions of a will; or 4. That the personal property is insufficient to pay both debts and charges, and a legacy effectual to pass or charge real estate; or 

5. That it is necessary, or for the best interests of the persons interested in the realty as devisees, legatees, or heirs, to sell it for the purpose of distribution.'

A petition for the sale of land belonging to a person under guardianship should set forth: 

1. That the personal property of such person is insufficient to pay his just debts and the charges of managing his estate; or 

2. That the personal property is insufficient to pay the expenses incurred by a county or the state in supporting or 

1. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15800; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13987 merely states that license may be granted when it shall appear to the court that the personalty is insufficient. See Ryder v. 

Flanders (1874) 30 Mich., 336, 342 stating that the petition must set forth facts sufficient to confer jurisdiction upon the court. 

Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Sec. 15800; C. L. (1915) Sec. 13987.

The wives of all the heirs should be made parties in sales for distribution in order to bar their dower. 2. 128

SALES 

129 caring for him, together with the charges of managing his estate; or 

3. That the income of his estate is insufficient to maintain him and his family, or to educate him (in case he is a minor) or to educate his children; or 

4. That it would be for his benefit to sell such realty and reinvest the proceeds.³

In framing the Judicature Act, several requirements found in earlier laws were dropped. For the purpose of the record, however, the petition should set forth some matters which are no longer made necessary by statute, but which good practice requires. The amount and nature of the personalty that was found in the estate, and what part of it remains at the time of filing the petition; the debts outstanding; a description of all of the real property in the estate, and the condition and value of each portion or lot. These were all required before 1915,* and should be included in the petition. 

And the petition should set forth any other facts concerning the estate which might help in presenting the full picture to the court and assist it in determining what parts, if any, of the realty should be sold.

The sale must be held between nine o'clock in the morning and sunset at some place in the county where the real estate located." The executor, administrator or guardian must file his report of the sale "immediately," and the court may examine into the proceedings; and if of the opinion that the proceedings were unfair, or the sum bid disproportionate to the value, and that a sum at least ten per cent greater, exclusive of the expenses of a new sale, could be obtained, may revoke the sale and order another one held."

Confirmation may not be made of the sale until at least eight days after the filing of the report, and objections may be filed to such confirmation; but if all the interested parties consent in writing, by attorney or guardian, or if the court is of the opinion that such delay would be injurious to the estate, confirmation may be made sooner. 24

The order of confirmation may be entered and conveyance directed to be made by the probate judge only upon satisfying himself that the sale was legally made and fairly conducted, and that the sum bid was not disproportionate to the value, or that no greater sum could be obtained."

28. Id. 29. 30. 

In Hoffman v. Beard" the administrator sold, under license of court, for the payment of debts, land of which he had not known until after closing the estate, and ten years after his appointment as administrator. The court held that the sale was void and that the probate court had no power to order it. The general rule was laid down that after the claims of creditors had been allowed, and the time for payment of debts had expired, the court lost jurisdiction to authorize the sale. 

The same court relaxed only three years later, however, and stated that exceptions might exist," as in cases in which the estate could not be settled and closed within the ordinary period. The rule in the Hoffman case was definitely abandoned in 1918 although the decision as to its facts was not overruled, and is still good authority. 31. 32. 33. 

Eberstein v. Oswalt (1881) 47 Mich. 254. 

Osman v. Traphagen (1871) 23 Mich. 80; Brown v. Hannah, (1908) 152 Mich. 33. An analogy may be found in sales under levy, and under foreclosure of mortgages, in which cases sales in parcels are required by statute. Mich. Comp. Laws (1929) Secs.

Sale After 

Estate Closed

SALES 

Sale Without

Court Order 

Lands which have been conveyed to an administrator for the benefit of the estate may be sold, and conveyed by the adfiling receipts showing this pro rata payment a quietus issue to the administrator. Nothing further was done until 1865 when the administrator discovered an interest in lands of which he had not before been informed, and filed his petition for license to sell this interest. The license was granted and sale was had. 

The purchaser brought ejectment. The supreme court held the sale void, stating: "We are of the opinion, therefore, that the accounts allowed in this case were not a proper and valid claim against the estate of Thomas Scott at the time the license to sell the property in question was granted; that the estate had previously been closed, and that the probate court had no power to order a sale of the real estate to pay debts already barred by our statutes." 

In Larzelere v. Starkweather (supra), the same Justice (Marston) said "That the general rule laid down and the conclusion reached in Hoffman v. Beard was correct, as the facts stood in that case, we have as yet seen no reason to doubt, but that exceptions to such general rule exist, where, after the expiration of the time there stated, the probate court has jurisdiction to order a sale of the real estate of the deceased, for the payment of his debts, we have no doubt. Cases may frequently arise under our statutes, owing to delays arising from causes over which the administrator and probate court have no control, where the estate cannot be closed and settled within the usual and ordinary period. In all such cases the probate court does not lose its jurisdiction, when properly invoked, to order a sale of the real estate . . . Hoffman v. Beard was followed in In re 

Young's Estate (1906) 144 Mich. 663, but the court did not discuss the theory at length. In In re Van Syckle's Estate, (supra) the question was again presented, and the court (Stone, J.) fully discussed the various cases (pp. 408-413) and stated: "We have already called attention to the cases which discarded the rule stated in the Hoffman case, and we are of the opinion that the other cases relied upon by the trial court and the appellees do not establish or support the rule contended for . . . In our opinion there is no statute of limitations to prevent the creditors of the estate from obtaining the delayed payment of their adjudicated claims, under the circumstances here existing. The estate remains open, and in the charge and control of the probate court." The last statement explains the holding: the Van Syckle estate was still open, while in the Hoffman case the estate had been previously closed.

Before sale an appraisal must be made as to the cash value by three disinterested persons. If the value is more than $1500.00 the appraisers must determine whether the property may be divided without injury, so that a homestead (including the dwelling house or appurtenances) not exceeding $1500.00 in value may be set apart, and the appraisers must file their report with the court." Personal Property 

There is no statutory provision requiring an executor or administrator to obtain license to sell personal property, and there is language in the cases to support the theory that such permission is unnecessary," although provision for obtaining permission is found in the statute."7 This act prescribes the manner in which such permission may be obtained, but does not make a court order indispensable. But if such permission is sought, the court's order must be strictly followed." 

If an executor or administrator may sell personal property without an order of court, it would certainly seem that he (1899) 122 Mich. 439, where the mortgage did not conform to the order as to amount, times when interest should be payable, terms of the mortgage, and provisions as to prior encumbrances, and was held to be invalid. This holding may not apply, however, to discrepancies in amount between the petition and the order. 

In Russell v. Wheeler (1901) 129 Mich. 41, a mortgage issued for an amount authorized, but in excess of the amount stated in the petition, wa sustained. The decision is of dubious value, however, Justice Grant stating that his brethren were doubtful as to the validity of the mortgage, but that the contestants would gain nothing by having it declared invalid, because of other facts which appeared in the case.

CHAPTER XII Appeals 

The legislature has provided for the taking of appeals to the circuit court from most' probate orders; and the 1931 Michigan Court Rules have specified the manner in which they are to be drawn and perfected.* A notice must be filed with the reasons for taking the appeal within twenty days after the entry of the order complained of, with the probate register."

A delayed appeal may be allowed by the circuit court, within such further time as may be permitted by law, upon motion and notice, and a showing ("by affidavit or otherwise") of merit and of the absence of culpable negligence on the part

CHAPTER XIII The Conclusiveness of Probate Decrees When Attacked Collaterally 

"Some courts are of General Jurisdiction, by which is meant that their authority extends to a great variety of matters; while others are only of special and limited jurisdiction, by which it is understood that they have authority extending only to certain specified cases. The want of jurisdiction is equally fatal in the proceedings of each; but different rules prevail in showing it. It is not to be assumed that a court of general jurisdiction has in any case proceeded to adjudge upon matters over which it had no authority; and its jurisdiction is to be presumed, whether there are recitals in its record to show it or not. On the other hand, no such intendment is made in favor of the judgment of a court of limited jurisdiction, but the recitals contained in the minutes of proceedings must be sufficient to show that the case was one which the law permitted the court to take cognizance of, and that the parties were subjected to its jurisdiction by proper process."" 

1. Cooley, Constitutional Limitations, 8th ed. pp. 858, 859. "The record of a court being silent on a jurisdictional point, it becomes of vital importance to know whether it is one of inferior or superior jurisdiction. On principle, it seems to me to be self evident that, if the court has unlimited jurisdiction over a class of cases, its jurisdiction in such matters is general; and that when its record shows such a case, all presumptions are in its favor and that silence is conclusive. According to this idea, the proceedings of probate courts, of county courts and county commissioners or supervisors in respect to county matters, boards for the assessment or revision of taxes, and justices of the peace in actions between landlord and tenant for the possession of land, being unlimited and generally exclusive, should be classed with those of superior courts, and all intendments made in their favor." Vanfleet, Collateral Attack on Judicial Proceedings, Sec. 811. 148

The Probate Court is a Court of General Jurisdiction 

In Palmer v. Oakley' the probate court was classified as a court of limited jurisdiction and in an action of ejectment, a probate sale of land, belonging to infants, was held void. 

The petition for the appointment of the guardian recited that the minors were under fourteen years of age. It did not appear of record whether the court found them to be under fourteen years of age as a matter of fact. Upon collateral attack it developed that one of the minors was over fourteen years of age. The record failed to show whether the minor was served with notice of the proceedings. The absence of an affirmative showing vitiated the decree; a presumption of regularity would not be indulged.³ 

In Shipman v. Butterfield the plaintiff, as administrator, brought an action of assumpsit. His authority was questioned by the defendant, because the petition for his appointment contained no allegation of his interest and therefore failed to invoke the jurisdiction of the court. The court said: (p. 

488) "It is true that the statute does not in express terms require that these facts shall be set out in the petition, but the rule is universal that jurisdiction must affirmatively appear; and whether the necessary facts are shown in one way or another, it is clear that in this case this general rule has been disregarded." 

2 Doug. (Mich.) 433 (1847). 

Idem. See p. 476 et seq. 

47 Mich. 487 (1882). 2. 3. 4. 

5. The plaintiff produced the whole file and records of the probate court for the purpose of sustaining its action, but they were "bare of any showing that the probate court had before it in any form the jurisdictional facts which authorized it to deal with the estate."

In Reason v. Jones, S. R., by his guardian, J. C., brought a bill to set aside a deed. The court said: (p. 673) "It is contended that the probate court did not acquire jurisdiction to appoint a guardian for (S. R.), because the citation was not served upon two of his children. Personal service was had upon (S. R.) and upon the citation was endorsed an admission of service . . . signed by his wife and four children. Three of them appeared. It is contended that there was no verification of the admission of service. The judge of probate was sworn upon this hearing and testified that this return of the officer, with the admission of service, was the only proof of service that was filed in the case. Whether any other proofs of service were introduced upon that hearing does not appear. The petition gave the court jurisdiction to proceed with the case. Of necessity, the court had to pass upon the regularity of the service. Probate courts are courts of general jurisdiction, and the regularity of their orders and decrees will be presumed, in the absence of evidence to the contrary." 

According to the best opinion this presumption is conclusive."

In the absence of a recital of proper service in the order of appointment-the existence of which did not appear in the case; and in view of the fact that the unverified admission of service was absolutely negative, the record in Reason v. 6. 7. it the indispensable matters (an authenticated copy of the foreign probate proceedings) when it assumed to act, we do not know. 

If it had, they were part of the subject-matter and should have appeared with the rest, but, as before tated, they did not so appear, and upon the face of the proof it would seem that there was no jurisdiction in the probate court to order the paper to be allowed as a will." It is submitted that the probate court order admitting the will, should have been competent evidence, because it did not appear affirmatively in the probate record that there was no authenticated copy of foreign probate, and therefore a presumption of regularity should have been indulged. 

119 Mich. 672 (1899).

See Moffitt v. McGrat, 25 Oregon 478, 480; 36 Pac. 578.

Jones was really silent upon the question of service; therefore, being a court of general jurisdiction, the law presumed that there had been proper service. Had it appeared affirmatively in the record that no service had been made, the record would have been bad upon its face and, obviously, no presumption could then have been indulged, in order to sustain the proceeding. It is submitted that, by its holding in Reason v. 

Jones, the court has discarded the doctrine announced in

The reason for the distinction between courts of general and limited jurisdiction, as concerns the conclusiveness of their judgments, is not clearly defined." Alger, in an article in the 

American Law Review," points out that the reason for the distinction grew out of the peculiar constitution of the English Courts, and suggests that it is not applicable in this country. He submits a test for our use: (p. 683) "Does the legislative act creating the court of limited jurisdiction, or granting a limited authority, whether the procedure prescribed is according to the course of the common law or of any other law, confer upon the court power to determine the collateral facts by which its jurisdiction is limited? In applying this test it would seem clear that, when there is a general authority to try cases particularly circumstanced, and the collateral point 10. 

See and compare Gillett v. Needham (1877) 37 Mich. 143.

It has been stated that even in courts of limited jurisdiction, a presumption of regularity will be indulged as to persons, process, evidence, etc., providing the allegations are complete as to subject matter, for which nothing will be presumed. See Dictum in 

Meyer v. Wilson (1906) 166 Ind. 651, 76 N. E. 748, 749; and

American and English Encyclopedia of Law, 2d ed. tit. jurisdiction, p. 1082. "The line of demarcation between courts of general and limited jurisdiction is not so definite, however, as is generally supposed. 

It is usual to state what particular courts fall within the one class and wha within the other, but what author has undertaken to mark with accuracy and precision the boundary between the two? Bacon has not, nor has Blackstone, nor any other elementary writer." Tucker v. Harris (1853) 13 Ga. 1, 7. 

11. "Conclusiveness of a Domestic Judgment as Affected by the 

Rank of the Court Which Rendered it." 33 Am. L. Rev. 665.

Distinction 

Between "General" and "Limited"

Historical 

Distinction limiting the jurisdiction of the court over the cause involves a question of fact, the power to settle the question, if not expressly granted, ought to be implied. The court is at a standstill and the end for which it is created is defeated if it cannot hear the evidence and decide the question. It is then deprived of all jurisdiction, because it has no means of determining the question on which its jurisdiction rests. The grant of power is general, namely, to hear and decide cases in a given state of facts. It is a familiar principle that a general power includes the particular powers essential to its execution. Therefore, by plain implication, the court has power to determine the facts upon which depends its jurisdiction of the cause. The implication is strengthened by obvious considerations of policy.

Freeman states that . . where the right of the court to assume jurisdiction depends upon the ascertainment of facts in pais and the court retains jurisdiction, a presumption arises, . . . when the validity of the judgment is attacked, that the necessary jurisdictional facts were proven.' 19 12 12. 13. 14. 15. 

(6 Some writers adopt the historical distinction which is based upon the scope of a court's authority." Others follow the case of Hahn v. Kelly," which held that a court of record was a court of general jurisdiction." 

Freeman, Judgments, 5th ed. Sec. 350. "The test of superiority was not the extent of territory over which the court exercised its authority, but the extent of its jurisdiction within its territorial limits. A court proceeding, according to the course of the common law, was an inferior court when its jurisdiction within its domain was limited materially, or in other words, did not extend to substantially all criminal or civil causes, but was confined to specific causes particularly circumstanced." 33 Am. L. Rev. 665, 669. "It would seem that the English Ecclesiastical Courts in respect to their authority over the estates of decedents were courts of inferior jurisdiction because they did not have jurisdiction of all such estates within their territorial limits . . . " Idem p. 670. 

See also Cooley's Constitutional Limitations, 8th ed. pp. 858, 859, quoted on page 148. 

34 Cal. 391, 94 Am. Dec. 742 (1868). "The use of the words 'Superior' and 'Inferior' or 'Limited'

Alger states that the "court of record" test is erroneous. (p. 665) "At common law, courts of record were either superior or inferior courts. Whether a court of justice was or was not technically of record ceased, in time, to be of consequence in the matter of the conclusiveness of its judgments, the rank of the court as superior or inferior, however, continued to be a test of such conclusiveness." 22 16 16. 

Probate courts in Massachusetts were made courts of record in 1862. However, the court was considered as of inferior jurisdiction, in the matter of the conclusiveness of its decrees until 1891, when, by statute, it was made a court of general 18 17. 18. 17 and 'General,' and 'Proceeding according to the course of the common law,' in the statement of the rule in question, however apt that may have once been, are less so at this time and place, and their duties, in view of our system and mode of procedure, would be better performed by the terms 'Courts of Record' and 'Courts and Tribunals not of record.'” 

See Freeman, Judgments, 5th ed. Sec. 374; Davis v. Hudson, (1881) 29 Minn. 27, Woerner, American Law of Administration, 3d ed. Sec. 145; also Justice Sherwood's opinion in Cahill v. 

Bassett (1887) 66 Mich. 407, 416. "The courts are of two kinds: Courts of record; Not of record, First, of courts of record, there is this diversity, viz: 

Supreme; Superior; Inferior." Hale, The History of the Common Law of England and an Analysis of the Civil Part of the 

Law, 6th ed. p. 24. "The probate courts of the several counties shall be courts of record." Mass. Stat. (1862) Ch. 68, Sec. 3. In the statutory revision of 1846 the Michigan probate court was made a court of record, Mich. Rev. Stat. (1846) Ch. 91, Sec. 2; Mich. Comp. 

Laws (1929) Sec. 13869; C. L. (1915) Sec. 12235; see Mich.

Const. Art. VII, Sec. 17. 

"Probate courts shall be courts of superior and general jurisdiction with reference to all cases and matters in which they have jurisdiction, and no order, decree, sentence, warrant, suit or process made, issued or pronounced by them need set out any adjudication or circumstances with greater particularity than would be required in other courts of superior and general jurisdiction, and the like presumption shall be made in favor of proceedings of the probate courts as would be made in favor of proceedings of other courts of superior and general jurisdiction." 

2 Mass. Gen. Laws (1921) C. 215, Sec. 2. 

Court of 

Record Test

Public Policy jurisdiction, entitled to the usual presumptions of regularity." 

Vanfleet states that courts are brought into disrepute by successful collateral attacks." Obviously, it is a matter of public policy to uphold the action of every court. If the historical factors which brought about a classification of courts in the matter of the conclusiveness of their judgments really do not exist in this country, as pointed out by Alger, it would seem desirable to uphold the action of all courts whether officially of record or not. It is submitted that the court of record test not only hampers the application of a broad public policy to some courts, but accomplishes its end by unnecessary indirection. If the judgment of a court is assailed collaterally, its action may be directly upheld by a presumption that it properly exercised its power-the contrary not appearing or it may be said that to deny the judgment would be to presume against the record. It is a well known principle that records, of courts officially of record, import absolute verity and cannot be successfully contradicted. Freeman says, "To conclude from a mere want or insufficiency of jurisdictional recitals in the record of a court of general jurisdiction that jurisdiction was not actually acquired would be, in effect to presume against the record, whereas it is a rule of general application that every intendment consistent with the record . . . will be indulged to sustain their proceedings and judgments. . . The absence from 19. 19 

Connors v. Cunard Steamship (1910) 204 Mass. 310, 322. 

See "Conclusiveness of Decrees of a Domestic Probate Court in Massachusetts," 13 Harv. L. Rev. 190. See reasons advanced by author for treating the Massachusetts' probate court as inferior. 

20. "Many people look upon the courts as places where jugglery and smartness are substituted for justice, and nothing tends more to increase their numbers than to see their property and rights, held under the sound adjudications of the courts, snatched away on account of some defect, or omission in a summons or return of service, or petition, which caused no actual harm to anyone. 

Such things tend to weaken law and order and to cause men to settle their rights by violence." Vanfleet, Collateral Attack, p. 3.

the record of the necessary jurisdictional facts will not overcome the presumption of jurisdiction . . . 9921 To uphold a judgment in order not to presume against an official record, as such, is to sustain the court's action by indirection. In other words the protection afforded a judgment comes as a by-product from the enforcement of another rule. Obviously, under this test, if a court is not of record, even though empowered to determine its own jurisdictional facts, its proceedings must show that such facts were ascertained to gain immunity from collateral attack."

The presumption that a court properly exercised its power in passing upon its own jurisdiction—the contrary not appearing-should be sufficient to sustain its action on collateral attack whether it be a court of record or not and whether its record be silent or not." 23 21. 22.

In the following Michigan cases the probate court has been classified as a court of general jurisdiction. The word "general," as distinguished from "limited" has been used in the historical sense. A court is of general jurisdiction when its authority extends to all probate matters within the state. 

If the historical basis is to be continued, there is ample author- 23. 

Freeman, Judgments, Sec. 383. "It is undeniable that if a court of limited jurisdiction is charged with the ascertainment of jurisdictional facts, and its record and proceedings show such facts were ascertained, they cannot be collaterally impeached." Freeman, Judgments, 5th ed. 

Sec. 350. "Some courts apparently think that there is a fundamental difference in the principle governing judgments of a court of limited or special, as distinguished from general jurisdiction. But the greater number of decisions in special proceedings are based upon the theory that, while jurisdiction is narrower in scope, the court is just as competent to determine matters that fall within its scope as is a court of general jurisdiction to determine the matters that fall within the scope of its jurisdiction. If the character of the subject-matter is such that it is included within the narrower jurisdiction, the court has power to make the inquiry, and its judgment is entitled to protection when attacked collaterally." "Collateral Attack upon Judgment because of Insufficiency of Pleadings," L. R. A. 1916 E. 316, 320, 321. 

Probate Court of General Jurisdiction

Review of Cases ity for indulging a presumption of regularity when the probate record fails to recite all jurisdictional matters. If the court of record test should be employed, the probate court has been entitled to the usual presumption of regularity since the adoption of the Revised Statutes of 1846, when it was made a court, officially of record. Likewise under the test suggested by Alger, it should be presumed that the Michigan probate court properly exercised its power in passing upon its own jurisdiction. It is quite clear, therefore, that decree of a Michigan probate court should be sustained in case the record is jurisdictionally imperfect. However, in none of the following cases do the records appear to be silent as to jurisdictional allegations, and it is possible that the conclusions would have been the same had the court been considered as of inferior jurisdiction. It is advisable to list these cases apart from Reason v. Jones, which at best is a rather weak authority, until the test for upholding a court's action is further clarified, either by statute or case law.

In Morford v. Dieffenbacker" a petition for the probate of a will was filed by the guardian of one of the beneficiaries under the will. It was objected that the guardian failed to prove his interest as alleged in the petition. In delivering the opinion of the court, Chief Justice Cooley said: (p. 605) "The probate courts of this state are courts of general and for the most part of exclusive jurisdiction in probate matters (citing People v. Wayne Circuit Court;" Woods v. Monroe;20 and Church v. Holcomb") and their orders and decrees are not to be collaterally attacked on any assumption that they have exercised their jurisdiction without evidence to support the allegations."

In Church v. Holcomb the court held valid a mortgage ordered by the probate court nine years after letters of admin- 24.

(and further, on p. 39) The probate court is one of general jurisdiction in testamentary and other probate matters; (citing 

People v. Wayne Circuit Court) and where no fatal defect appears on the face of the proceedings, its action in such matters is not void." ,, 29

Special Statutory Power

Subject Matter

Subject Matter 

While presumptions of regularity will be indulged when the jurisdictional recitals are incomplete, it must appear from an examination of the record that the subject-matter came within the jurisdiction of the court."

The use of the term "subject-matter" in connection with jurisdiction has been defined: "A court is said to have jurisdiction over the subject-matter when it has the power to hear, try and determine some right, not in a particular case but in every case of that class.' 

9 45

A court, in deciding whether the subject-matter comes within its jurisdiction, may be faced with a "pure question of fact; the application of a legal standard to conceded facts; or or the applicability of rules of law limiting the jurisdiction . . . of a particular court. .; or . . . [the] application of a . . rule of substantive law." 

9 46 43. 44. 45. 46. 47. 

9 43 .

In an excellent note in The Lawyer's Reports Annotated" the author states that it need not of necessity appear in the pleadings that the subject-matter came within the jurisdiction of the court, but that it may be gathered from the entire record. 

He said: (pp. 322-324) "Does the court have jurisdiction of the subject-matter when the pleadings are not sufficient to state a cause of action? Of course, the courts. have directly held that it has. But while the courts and text writers have with practical unanimity held to this view, it has lately been questioned by one writer (Hughes, Law Restated). * * * 

Freeman, Judgments, 5th ed. Sec. 389. See Galpin v. Page 

18 Wall (U. S.) 350, 21 L. ed. 959, wherein the doctrine was considered at length. 

Vanfleet, Collateral Attack, Secs. 59, 60, 61. "Subject-matter" 9 Col. L. Rev. 419, 423. 

40 Harv. L. Rev. 766, 769, n. 26. "Collateral Attack upon Judgment Because of Insufficiency of 

Pleadings." L. R. A. 1916 E. 316.

The view opposing that of the court would seem to be that the function of the pleadings is to confer jurisdiction on the court; that the court can not take the initial step of inquiring into the question of its own jurisdiction until the pleadings have clearly brought the matter within its jurisdiction; hence, unless a cause of action is stated in the pleadings, the subjectmatter is not brought within the jurisdiction. ***So, it is held that the court has no jurisdiction unless there is something to start the judicial mind in motion. But the term jurisdiction has here a peculiar meaning. The jurisdiction is there and the subject-matter is within it, but it is latent until called. 

The batteries are charged, the circuit is complete, and the bell will ring on the slightest touch of the button. This is wholly different if the bell is in a different circuit, as is the case when the subject-matter is not within the general domain of the court's power. So, where the subject-matter is within the general jurisdiction of the court in this broad sense, any call upon the court, however imperfect, is sufficient to start the judicial mind upon inquiry. "The technical pleadings may or may not constitute the call. 

Where they do constitute a call, that service is a mere incident to the real function of the pleadings, as is shown by the fact that the call is frequently made, and the judicial mind is in motion, before any pleadings are filed. Since, as already shown, the court's jurisdiction, where the subject matter falls within its scope, exists even before the call is made, and no call, however loud and perfect, can give it jurisdiction if the subject-matter is not within its scope, it follows that the incidental service of starting the judicial mind to work, sometimes performed by the pleadings, is not a vital factor in determining whether or not the court has jurisdiction of the subject matter in the broad sense. That question is dependent upon the character of the inherent facts, which may be gathered from the whole record or any part thereof, including the judgment itself. (p. 326) “A court, in dismissing a cause on the ground that the complaint does not state a cause of action, is merely judging the cause on its merits from plaintiff's point of view in order to save time, while sustaining a collateral attack on a

Court May Exceed

Authority judgment for the same cause would involve a review of the prior decision on the merits. This would result in so much uncertainty and injustice that titles would be insecure, property unsafe, and the law in chaos."

A court, having jurisdiction of the subject-matter, and of the parties, may transcend its authority and make void its judgment. In Windsor v. McVeigh," the court said: "Though the court may possess jurisdiction of a cause, of the subjectmatter, and of the parties, it is still limited in its modes of procedure and in the extent and character of its judgments. 

It must act judicially in all things, and cannot then transcend the power conferred by the law. If, for instance, the action be upon a money demand, the court notwithstanding its complete jurisdiction over the subject and parties, has no power to pass judgment of imprisonment in the penitentiary upon the defendant. If the action be for a libel or personal tort, the court cannot order in the case a specific performance of a contract. If the action be for the possession of real property, the court is powerless to admit in the case the probate of a will." 97 49
