"Their decision was the exercise of jurisdiction, which was confessed by the representation, for whenever that was before the court, they must hear and determine whether that was true or not; it was a subject on which there might be judicial action. "The subsequent provisions of the act of Michigan relate exclusively to acts and proceedings in the execution of the order of sale, or are directory to the administrator to accompany the representation with a certificate of the judge of probate, and to the court, before passing on such representation, to order notice to be given to the parties concerned, to show cause why the license should not be granted; but these provisions do not affect the jurisdiction of the court, they apply only to its exercise."

In Florentine v. Barton," Grignon's Lessee v. Astor was cited with approval. An Illinois statute authorized the sale of a decedent's real estate when the personal estate was insufficient for the payment of debts, and provided that an account of the debts and personal estate should accompany the petition and that notice should be given the heirs (the act, being full, minute, and stringent, in this regard) with a copy of the account and petition. A private act of the legislature authorized a private sale. The record of the State Circuit Court, which ordered the sale, recited a petition setting forth that the personal estate was not sufficient to pay debts and praying an order to sell certain parts of the real estate for the purpose of paying them, agreeably to the private act of the legislature already referred to, and concluding with an order that the administrators should sell an item described. There was no mention whatever in the record that any notice had been given to the heirs or others, or any mention of the nature or amount of the indebtedness.

The court said: "By the law of Illinois, the lands of one deceased are liable for the payment of his debts. The Circuit 

Court of the county in which the administration is granted has jurisdiction to order their sale for that purpose. The petition of the administrator, setting forth that the personal property of the deceased is insufficient to pay such debts, and praying the court for an order of sale, brought the case fully within the jurisdiction of the court. 56 56.

Protective Statute of 1838 Not Essential if Probate Court is of General Jurisdiction 

Formerly in Massachusetts it was the rule that the minutes of a probate proceeding should disclose in detail every particular required by statute, or else its decree would be void." Many purchasers at probate sales unfairly lost their titles because of some trivial technicality. In 1838 Massachusetts enacted a protective statute and the following-a substantial copy--was passed by the Michigan legislature: Sec. 15837 "In case of an action relating to any estate sold by an executor, administrator or guardian, in which an heir or other person claiming under the deceased, or in which the ward or any person claiming under him, shall contest the validity of the sale, it shall not be avoided on account of any irregularity" in the proceeding, provided it shall appear: 58. of Assembly, as to the manner of sale, was within the constitutional power of the legislature; and that all the provisions of the law, as to notices which are directory to the administrators, have been complied with. "The court having a right to decide every question which occurs in a cause, whether its decision be correct or otherwise, its judgment, until reversed, is binding on every other court.' The purchaser, under such a sale is not bound to look further back than the order of the court, or to inquire as to its mistakes. The court is not bound to enter on record the evidence on which any fact was decided. The proceedings on which the action of the court is grounded, are usually kept on separate papers, which are often mislaid or lost. A different doctrine would (especially after a lapse of over thirty years) render titles under a judicial sale worthless, and a 'mere trap for the unwary.' These propositions will be found discussed at length and fully decided by us in Grignon's Lessee v. Astor. 

Any further argument in vindication of them would be superfluous." 57.

The solution of the question of jurisdiction depends upon the sufficiency of the jurisdictional allegations. What constitutes a sufficient jurisdictional allegation is discussed in the case of

Section 13964 of the Compiled Laws of 1929 provides that no person shall bring or maintain any action for the recovery of any lands within five years, where the defendant claims title through a deed made upon a sale by an executor, administrator, guardian, or testamentary trustee, or by a sheriff, or other proper ministerial officer under a judgment, decree, or process of a court of competent jurisdiction.

In Toll v. Wright (1877) 37 Mich. 93 the court held that the above act only protected sales in which it appeared that the court had jurisdiction. The court did not decide upon the sufficiency of the jurisdictional allegations appearing in the probate record. 

It held that under the following statute it was immaterial whether the court had or had not jurisdiction to make the sale. "No action for the recovery of any estate sold by an executor or administrator under the provisions of this chapter, shall be maintained by an heir or other person claiming under the deceased testator or intestate, unless it be commenced within five years next after the sale; and no action for any estate sold in like manner by a guardian shall be maintained by the ward, or by any person claiming under him, unless it be commenced within five years next after the termination of the guardianship, except as hereinafter provided." C. L. (1871) Sec. 4594; C. L. (1897) Sec. 9127.

It must be considered that the most liberal construction of the language in favor of such purchaser and against the heir was intended."

In Davis v. Hudson (1881) 29 Minn. 27, 33, 34, 35, the court held that this statute relaxes the rule applied to a court of general jurisdiction and permits the sale of land to be assailed collaterally where the presumption without the statute would have sustained the proceedings. 

In Averill v. Jackson City Bank (1897) 114 Mich. 20 Justice Statute

Defeats Purpose

In Palmer v. Oakley," in discussing a sale that took place before the adoption of the statute, the court said: (pp. 495, 496) "The statute required, that, before making sale of any real estate by a guardian, a bond shall be given with securities and thirty days' notice of the intended sale. An oath is also required. The requirement in respect to the bond and notice may be considered as a limitation or restriction upon the authority to sell. But does the neglect on the part of the guardian to comply with these several provisions of the statute, render the sale absolutely void, and can it affect the rights of an innocent bona fide purchaser, claiming through the decree authorizing the sale? All that a purchaser at a judicial sale is bound to look to with a view to his protection, is to see that the court by whom the sale was authorized was empowered to make the decree. If the court had the power, the failure of the guardian, as in this case, to fulfill certain directions which the law imposed on her, should not, and can not prejudice the rights acquired by such purchaser." If the court in Palmer v. Oakley, believing the probate court to be of limited jurisdiction, thought these matters were not jurisdictional, it is quite clear that their absence should be of no consequence now that the court is considered one of general jurisdiction, in which presumptions of regularity are indulged.®

It is submitted that the statute hinders rather than accomplishes the purpose which prompted its enactment, namely, the protection of sales. 

Montgomery in a dissenting opinion said: (p. 27) "It is undoubtedly true, in a sense, that the probate court is a court of general jurisdiction; but the proceeding for the sale of lands by an executor or administrator is statutory, and it cannot be doubted that it would be competent for the legislature to fix the limit of the presumptions that are to arise from an adjudication of the court, or to limit the conclusiveness of the holding of the court upon any question." 

62. 2 Doug. (Mich.) 433 (1847). 

63. See Vanfleet on Collateral Attack upon Judicial Proceedings,

Chap. VIII.

Section 2 of the statute provides for the giving of a bond in case a bond is required upon granting a license. 

As suggested in Palmer v. Oakley, and by Vanfleet, the absence of a bond should not invalidate the proceedings.

In administration sales for debts, the court has construed the statute leniently, while in guardianship sales the protection of the infant has been considered of prime importance, and the provision requiring a bond has been strictly construed. 

This strict construction for the benefit of the minor is unusual, because the statute was designed primarily to protect the buyer."

In Steward v. Bailey," a guardian's sale was attacked collaterally because no bond was given. It appeared that none had been required by the probate judge. The court held that even though the wording of the statute appeared to make the giving of a bond discretionary with the probate judge, nevertheless, from an examination of the whole chapter, it appeared that a bond was an absolute essential and that no discretion was intended to be lodged with the judge in his respect." In Coon v. Fry," in a collateral attack, it was argued that an administrator's sale for debts was void, because, among other things, no bond was given by the administrator. The court upheld the sale because it appeared that no bond had been required by the judge of probate. See Norman v. Olney, wherein the court said: (p. 563) "The neglect of the probate court to require a bond should not be allowed to affect the rights of an innocent purchaser under an executor's sale in a collateral suit, after such sale has been duly confirmed by the probate court. The statute was evidently framed for the purpose of protecting sales of this kind, as far as possible, where the sale of the premises had been confirmed and the lands are held by persons purchasing at such sale in good 68 

64. See note 61, p. 169. 65. 

28 Mich. 251 (1873). 

66. See Ryder v. Flanders (1874) 30 Mich. 336 and Fender v. Powers (1886) 62 Mich. 324.

Notice faith. It must be considered that the most liberal construction of the language in favor of such person and against the heir was intended."

In Woods v. Monroe," the court divided upon the question of whether the evidence was sufficient to show that the administrator gave a bond upon the sale. Justices Cooley and Campbell were satisfied by the evidence taken in the proceedings to quiet title that the administrator had given a bond in the probate court sale, while Justices Christiancy and Graves were unconvinced. The decree of the lower court upholding the sale, was consequently confirmed by a division of the court. 

Section 3 of the statute concerns the oath which is to be taken as prescribed in the sales chapter. 

The sales statute formerly provided that an oath be taken before fixing on the time and place of sale." In Ryder v. 

Flanders," the guardian took an oath only a few days before the sale. The court said: (p. 343) "This oath, when taken, was not in my view one either authorized or required by law, and is therefore extra-judicial, and as it does not afford the safeguard intended by the statute, it cannot, within the meaning of the third sub-division of the 23rd section, be treated as 'the oath prescribed in this chapter.'"

Section 4 of the statute provides that a notice of the time and place of sale must be given as prescribed in the sales statute. In Schaale v. Wasey," the notice of sale, though written in English, was published in a newspaper printed in the 

German language. The court said: (p. 420) "We do not think such a publication, as the one in this case, a proper one under our laws, but we are not prepared to say that it vitiates a good faith sale legally made in all other respects, especially when such sale is attacked collaterally and there is no evidence showing a want of bidders at such sale or any inadequacy of 69.

price in the sale." It was also objected that the notices were not all posted in the ward in which the property was situated. 

The court said: (p. 420) "We find, however, no evidence in the record showing in what ward this property was located or in what wards the places mentioned in the affidavit were situated at the time of the posting of such notices. We can not take judicial notice that any certain building in the City of Detroit is situated in a certain ward. There being no proof upon this subject, it must be presumed that the judge of probate had proper evidence before him that the requisite notices under the statute were posted in the ward in which the land was located, as he confirmed the sale. At least we can not go outside of the record to ascertain that the notices were not posted in the proper ward after a lapse of nearly twenty years when the boundaries of the wards are frequently changed, and that too, by local action or local laws." 

In Averill v. Jackson City Bank," an administrator's sale was upheld upon collateral attack, against the objection that the notices of sale were posted in the three most public places just outside the ward, rather than within the ward, in which the property was situated, as prescribed by the statute. Justice 

Montgomery dissented upon the theory that the legislature intended by the statute to limit the presumptions that arise from an adjudication of the court, or to limit the conclusiveness of the holding of a court upon any question. 

Evidence Allowed to Support Imperfect Record 

Sometimes the written record of a probate sale is lost. 

In Blanchard v. DeGraff," the court held, as stated in the syllabus: "In a case like this, the questions are, was the sale licensed, the bond given, the oaths taken, the notice of sale given, and the premises sold at public auction, and are they now held by one who purchased them in good faith? These conditions must be established by the best evidence and by the record when the record exists and can be obtained. If not, then the next best evidence may be resorted to. If the record or original papers have been lost or destroyed without fault 

73. 114 Mich. 20 (1897). 

74. 60 Mich. 107 (1886).

or fraud on the part of the defendant, their contents may be given in evidence as in other cases."

In Drake v. Kinsell," the record of the sale was lost and there remained merely the minute book of the judge. The judge was examined as a witness and testified that all the proceedings took place. The court held that all four requisites required by the statute appeared, and the sale was sustained.

In Woods v. Monroe," the Supreme Court divided upon the sufficiency of the evidence introduced in a proceeding to quiet title, as proving that a bond had been given by the administrator in a probate sale. In Persinger 

In Persinger v. Jubb," the court held that a sufficient publication of a notice of a guardian's sale may be shown by other evidence than the affidavit therefore, which is for the purpose of a permanent record. In Egan v. Grece, no license appeared in the record of the probate court. However, it appeared that a license was granted by the recitals in the affidavit, bond, report of sale, and the deed.” 

Solution of Other Questions Arising in Collateral Attack

Upon Probate Sales 

78 80 In Cahill v. Bassett a probate sale was sustained as held in the lower court, because the Supreme Court divided upon the propriety of the following:

A petition was presented to the probate court for the sale of land in order that the debts might be paid. The prayer for a sale was refused, because of a depressed real estate market, though the court gave a license to mortgage the land. 

Nothing having been done under this license, and after a few months, an order was entered authorizing a sale—though no new petition was filed nor any notice given. The notice of sale made when the first petition was presented was only published for five, instead of the required six weeks. Justice 

Campbell said (Sherwood, J. concurring) (p. 416): "There is no reason, I apprehend, but that the court had the power to 

75. 38 Mich. 232 (1878). 76. 

17 Mich. 237 (1868). 77. 

52 Mich. 304 (1883). 78. 

79 Mich. 629 (1890). 

79. See Dexter v. Cranston (1879) 41 Mich. 448. 80. 

66 Mich. 407 (1887).

continue the matter for final order in the premises as he did, or as the court says he did in the order. I think this recital must prevail in the absence of any testimony to the contrary. 

The probate court is one of record and its orders and decrees should have full credence in collateral proceedings where they come in issue, in a case wherein the court has acquired jurisdiction." No notice of hearing for the sale, after refusal of the first petition, was necessary, because as said by the justices (p. 416): "Our probate practice does not require any notice of hearing upon petitions, other than the first one given for that purpose, on continuance of the matter, and none was necessary in this case for the hearing on the 11th of November." Justice Champlin said (Morse J. concurring) (p. 419): "I do not think that the probate court obtained jurisdiction to license the sale of the real estate under the petition. The judge refused the petition to sell, but authorized the petitioner to mortgage. He had authority to refuse the license to sell, but no authority to grant a license to mortgage without a petition for that purpose. The court could not afterwards, without a new petition and notice to all interested, act upon the prayer of the petition, which he had refused, and grant a license to sell. Neither do I think that the defective notice of sale could be cured by adjournment. It is not a compliance with the statute, which was designed for the protection of parties interested, and to induce competition in bidding at the sale."

In Showers v. Robinson," it was held that a homestead could not be sold for debts and that a purchaser could not seek protection under the statute of limitations, because the homestead right exists, regardless of any sale. 

In Lynch v. Kirby," the guardian died after the probate court had confirmed a sale and ordered a conveyance. It was held that the succeeding guardian could complete the transaction. As stated in the syllabus: "The act of conveyance is rather official than personal and more a function of the place than a matter appropriated to any individual.”
